To read the originals: Maslow's paper can be read in full in York University's Classics in the History of Psychology. The need for self-actualization is on p.382.

</details>

<!--LENS:Viewpoint 2|Anxiety, and faith and idealization, seen from the viewpoint of psychological defense mechanisms-->

As seen in the chapter "[The mind has mechanisms to protect itself](#ldd-defense)", the mind has moves that protect it from anxiety and pain, and psychoanalysis has called them defense mechanisms. Freud wrote of religious ideas from the side of wishes arising from human helplessness (1927), and Klein counts idealization among the early moves that protect against anxiety (1946). In the three chapters that follow, we first look at what anxiety is, then at faith and idealization from the side of the moves that protect one's own weakness and the anxiety that wells up there.

<!--/LENS-->

## What is anxiety?

<!--OPEN-->

<a id="ldd-uncertain"></a>The words on self-acceptance in the previous chapter included "to be precise, anxiety does arise, but one can be with the anxiety". Is anxiety the opposite of reassurance?

When you finally pick up the envelope with your health check results, which you have left unopened for weeks, to open it, the anxiety is at its strongest. Once you open it and read the contents, the anxiety has sometimes eased, even if the results are not good. The anxiety grew stronger not when bad news came, but when you began to face what you had not been looking at.

While thinking about where in this reader to treat the anxiety of not being able to see ahead, pjdhiro said this.

<strong>pjdhiro's view (translated by Claude):</strong>The feeling of anxiety is very important. It is not the opposite of reassurance either.

On what anxiety is, pjdhiro cites one definition from the theory of the psychiatrist Shinya Nitta.

<strong>pjdhiro's view (translated by Claude):</strong>I like Shinya Nitta's definition: something like the dissociation that happens when returning from a state of psychological defense to facing reality.

Nitta writes that "anxiety is precisely dissociation". Dissociation in Nitta's sense is a movement exactly opposite to the work that protects the mind (defense): the protection peels away, and the unpleasant self that had been protected comes out. Nitta writes that the two go back and forth, as "defense ⇔ dissociation", and writes of the movement of the protection peeling away as something everyone has. Read with this definition, anxiety wells up where the protection comes undone and one begins to face what one had not been looking at. The faith and idealization seen in this group are moves on the side of that protection.

Other theories have also defined anxiety. Freud wrote of it as a signal that gives warning of danger in advance, May as a threat to the values that support oneself as a person, and Shoma Morita as the reverse side of the desire to live better. Many theories, like Nitta, separate anxiety from fear, write of it as something without a fixed object, and see anxiety as an important working. How it relates to reassurance differs by theory. The side of attachment writes that anxiety turns into comfort when one comes near protection, and Morita wrote that only by living in the mind that is always uneasy is there great reassurance.

A page that starts from the definition of anxiety and lines up its difference from fear, the definitions of other theories, whether anxiety or protection comes first, whether one returns to protection or keeps facing, and how what anxiety faces differs by stage, is placed as the separate page "[What is anxiety?](anxiety.html)" (in Japanese). The definitions of other theories are in its chapter "[How have other theories defined anxiety?](anxiety.html#ほかの論は不安をどう定義してきたか)" (in Japanese).

<details><summary>More — how does anxiety well up when one cannot see ahead? (research on preparation and the feeling of safety)</summary>

When one cannot see ahead, the body first prepares, research has written. Some research grasps anxiety from the side of the uncertainty of future threat and of the workings that prepare for it (Grupe and Nitschke 2013). Some research also holds that under uncertainty the stress response is not born after something happens, but is working from the start, and is held down only while one feels safe (Brosschot, Verkuil and Thayer 2018). In this reading, the signal of survival is not peculiar to some person or some stage of growth, but comes back to anyone once safety can no longer be felt.

Where does the feeling of safety come from? The same research counts, as one source of the feeling of safety, a strong network of connections for people and for animals that live in groups (Brosschot, Verkuil and Thayer 2018). From here, the relation of the two signals can be re-read in one form: writing the signal of survival as what comes back when safety cannot be felt, and the signal of trust as the feeling of safety that holds it down. ["What Is Consciousness"](https://uminomae.github.io/project-design-theory/as/) writes the two signals as two directions. Whether they are two separate signals, or one preparedness and what holds it down, touches the root of how "What Is Consciousness" writes, so it has not been decided yet. Either way, the two are in the body at the same time and are not cut off from each other.

Grupe and Nitschke (2013) hold that uncertainty makes it difficult to prepare ahead and that this leads to anxiety, and describe moves to prepare for the uncertainty of future threat as adaptive while they are proportionate to the likelihood and severity of the threat.

Brosschot, Verkuil and Thayer (2018) placed the stress response as a default that is always on and is held down by the prefrontal cortex only when safety is felt. When safety can no longer be felt, that holding down comes off at once. They also write that the holding down coming off does not cause the stress response but allows it. As things that damage the feeling of safety, they name loneliness, low social status, urban environments and adversity in early childhood, and count a strong network of connections as one source of safety.

There is also a foothold for the reading that if not seeing ahead is held as a state known to be unseen, alarms are less likely to sound. Yu and Dayan (2005) separate the uncertainty of knowing that cues are unreliable within a familiar situation (expected uncertainty) from the uncertainty caused by an unannounced switch of situation (unexpected uncertainty), and propose assigning them to separate systems of neural modulation. In people with more than ten thousand hours of meditation, activity in the insula and amygdala was lower before pain (in the anticipation phase), while during pain activity in the insula and cingulate was, if anything, stronger; pain intensity was reported the same as by novices, and unpleasantness as less (Lutz et al. 2013). It is not that the signal stops sounding; it does not sound in the anticipation phase, and sounds when the event comes.

<!--REF:Nerves and emotion-->

Sources: [Grupe & Nitschke (2013)](https://doi.org/10.1038/nrn3524) "Uncertainty and anticipation in anxiety", *Nature Reviews Neuroscience* 14(7):488 (full text checked against [the authors' manuscript on PMC; the passages on uncertainty and preparing in the abstract and introduction](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4276319/)) / [Brosschot, Verkuil & Thayer (2018)](https://doi.org/10.3390/ijerph15030464) "Generalized Unsafety Theory of Stress", *International Journal of Environmental Research and Public Health* 15(3):464 (full text checked against [the public version on PMC](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5877009/); abstract, section 1.2, section 3) / [Yu & Dayan (2005)](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.neuron.2005.04.026) "Uncertainty, neuromodulation, and attention", *Neuron* 46(4):681 (full text checked against [the PDF on the author's site](https://web.archive.org/web/20051024185924/http://www.gatsby.ucl.ac.uk:80/~dayan/papers/yud2005.pdf); abstract and introduction on p.681) / [Lutz, McFarlin, Perlman, Salomons & Davidson (2013)](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.neuroimage.2012.09.030) "Altered anterior insula activation during anticipation and experience of painful stimuli in expert meditators", *NeuroImage* 64:538 (full text checked against [the authors' manuscript on PMC; abstract and methods](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3787201/)).

</details>

## How do faith and trust differ?

<!--OPEN-->

The chapter "[How do expectation and trust differ?](#ldd-expect)" saw expectation as a feeling picturing what will happen in the direction one hopes. Hoping also enters into believing. How did psychoanalysis see a way of believing like faith?

Harold Camping, who preached the Bible on American radio, declared that on 21 May 2011 Christ would come again and true believers would be taken up into heaven, and his network announced it to the world. Nothing happened on that day, nor on 21 October, which he had named as another important day. In November of that year, Camping wrote on the network's site that God had guided us to the two days, but that we had not understood the meaning of those days at all, and that he did not believe the Biblical calendar was wrong. This is a re-reading in which what failed was not the date, but how the date was taken. It was in March of the following year that Camping admitted he had been wrong about the timing, and wrote that the declaration had been an incorrect and sinful statement.

<a id="ldd-faith"></a>Freud called a belief an illusion when, among its motives, the fulfilment of a wish comes to the fore (1927). Freud also wrote that an illusion is not the same as an error, and may later turn out right. What Freud separated was not whether what is believed is correct, but where the way of believing comes from. On that basis Freud wrote that an illusion dispenses with verifying itself. In the same book Freud also writes that the wish-illusions of religion are accompanied by a move that does not acknowledge reality. It is one of the mind's defenses, which psychoanalysis has called denial (the chapter "[The mind has mechanisms to protect itself](#ldd-defense)"; for its place among the levels, see the table on the separate page "[What are psychological defense mechanisms?](defense-mechanisms.html#防衛の種類と水準)" (in Japanese)). Camping's re-reading in November has the shape of this denial. When the failure is moved over to the side of a "meaning" that cannot be checked, what was believed can no longer fail. pjdhiro sees denial as found quite often, not only in matters of faith. By contrast, Stockdale in the chapter "[How do expectation and trust differ?](#ldd-expect)" did not let go of the belief that he would get out, and did not look away from the harsh reality.

Freud does not call faith by the name of a single defense. In the same book he places at the root of faith the helplessness of childhood, which arouses the wish for protection through love, and the father who has answered that wish. He writes that knowing the helplessness lasts throughout life leads people to keep holding that there is a more powerful father. He likens religion to a universal obsessional neurosis of humankind arising from the relation to the father, and immediately afterwards adds that this comparison does not exhaust what religion is. The move of picturing a more powerful father overlaps with idealization in the next chapter. But Freud's illusion is defined by where the way of believing comes from (a wish), while idealization is defined by what one looks at in the other (only the good side). This reader does not treat the two as the same, and lines them up in the next chapter.

This chapter does not settle on one meaning of trust. Whether people can tell trust apart from other feelings such as faith was seen in the chapter "[Why are feelings similar to trust hard to tell apart?](#ldd-discern)".

Denial is found quite often.").

## How do idealization and trust differ?

<!--OPEN-->

Expectation is also a move of counting on the other as almighty, as seen in the chapter "[How do expectation and trust differ?](#ldd-expect)". Faith includes a way of believing in which the wish comes to the front, as seen in the chapter "[How do faith and trust differ?](#ldd-faith)". Both contain the move of choosing to see only the good side. Is seeing only the good side the same as trusting the other?

In Act 3 of Shakespeare's *Othello*, the general Othello, right after seeing his wife Desdemona leave the room, says that when he no longer loves her, chaos will come again. What overturned an image that great was a single invention by his subordinate Iago. Iago says that he saw the lieutenant Cassio wipe his beard today with the lady's handkerchief. At that moment the handkerchief was in Iago's own hands. With that alone, Othello says he has blown all his love away to heaven, and calls up black vengeance. Desdemona has betrayed nothing, and in the final act Othello loses that wife by his own hand. An image built only of good parts had no place to keep even one bad side, and a single invention turned over the whole image.

On a page that made the five stages of creation into a diagram, pjdhiro named this move as a phenomenon to be especially careful of, and later restated it like this.

<a id="ldd-idealize"></a><strong>pjdhiro's view (translated by Claude):</strong>Like Nonaka's three excesses, anything in excess is as bad as falling short. Self-destruction through excessive "good" is a phenomenon I feel around the various things that pass for "correct answers": one easily judges the fluctuation on the opposite side as "bad", chooses only the "good", and goes to excess and becomes fixed. Examples: justice, perfection, affection, value, being creative, knowledge, diversity, joy, power, peace.

On the same day, pjdhiro went on like this.

<strong>pjdhiro's view (translated by Claude):</strong>I feel that even confident, idealistic policies have self-destruction through excessive "good".

Psychoanalysis has called the movement of selecting only the good aspects and seeing the opposite aspects split off idealization. Klein counted idealization among the early moves that protect against anxiety, and at the same time wrote that it is part of normal development (1946). The infant holds the satisfying object and the frustrating object apart, and protects itself from the terrifying aspect by enlarging the good aspect. What pjdhiro's fixing of "good", Klein's idealization and Nitta's *ate* have in common is a movement of selecting and enlarging the good aspect and keeping the opposite side split off.

Klein also writes that as the infant grows, the loved and hated aspects come to be felt as not so far apart as before. What wells up then is fear of loss, a strong sense of guilt and feelings close to mourning, and the urge to repair the one it feels it has harmed (reparation) comes to the fore. As anxiety weakens, Klein continues, the object is both less idealized and less terrifying. In Klein's writing, idealization and the anger that follows it form one pair. What is included in the definition of idealization goes only as far as enlarging the good aspect and keeping the bad aspect split off; anger is not included. But the split-off bad aspect does not vanish; it is held separately, as the image of a terrifying object. That is why idealization and terror grow stronger together and weaken together, and it can be read that when a single bad mark enters the good image, the object easily moves as a whole over to the terrifying side that had been split off. Othello's anger overlaps with this way of moving over.

This page does not settle on one difference between idealization and trust. Set beside idealization, one aspect of trust comes into view. The working notes of "What Is Consciousness" take, as one of the axes for looking at trust, the side that sees only good outcomes and the side that holds good and bad together. On this axis, idealization is on the former side. Which side trust is on is not yet decided, since what trust is is still being searched for.

<!--LENS:Viewpoint 3|Reassurance, dependence and attachment, seen from the viewpoint of theories of the mother–child relationship-->

Winnicott and Bowlby wrote, from the relationship between an infant and the person who cares for it, about what people count on and by what they feel protected. These are theories on the side that loosens protection, like empathy and holding (the chapter "[In what situations does the mind's defense loosen?](#ldd-holding)"). In the two chapters that follow, reassurance and dependence, and attachment, are seen from the side of this relationship.

<!--/LENS-->

## How do reassurance and dependence differ from trust?

<!--OPEN-->

Idealization was a movement to protect oneself from anxiety. Then is the feeling of being protected, of relying on someone with reassurance, the same as trust?

There is a view that being able to stand on one's own feet without relying on others is the condition of adult relationships of trust, and that dependence is something to grow out of quickly. Harlow took infant monkeys from their mothers soon after birth, raised them beside a doll mother wrapped in cloth, and put them into an unfamiliar room in which unfamiliar objects had been placed (1958). When the cloth mother was there, the infant monkey first ran to her and clung to her, then after a while left her to go and examine the objects, came back to the mother again, and then went out into the unfamiliar world. When the cloth mother was not there, the infants often crouched and froze, and movements such as crying out, rocking the body and sucking a finger suddenly increased. The infants went out into the unfamiliar room of their own accord when there was someone they could quickly return to and cling to.

Because there is someone to return to, one can go out into unfamiliar places. Around the time of writing the sister reader "Isn't Love like gravity?", pjdhiro looked back on a year of working with AI using the image of the umbilical cord (the chapter "[Isn't Love like gravity?](#ldd-center)") like this.

<a id="ldd-safety"></a><strong>pjdhiro's view (translated by Claude):</strong>For me, this year of collaborating with AI was exactly a sense of reassurance between me and a boundless AI, the brain of the world. Connected by an umbilical cord, entrusting my arguments with reassurance.

Winnicott wrote that the chief characteristic of infancy is dependence (1960). Wherever there is an infant there is always someone caring for it, and at first infant and care are one unit that cannot be separated. Winnicott called not only physically holding the infant but the whole of the provision surrounding the infant "holding", and wrote that it is reliable, not mechanically, but in a way that includes empathy for the other. Dependence is at first a dependence in which the infant is not even aware of the care, and later becomes a dependence aware of needing care. From there, Winnicott writes, as memories of care accumulate and a feeling of being able to rely on the environment (confidence) grows, the infant moves in the direction of managing without actual care.

In Winnicott's way of writing, relying and being held is the foothold for the feeling of being able to rely. Dependence can be read as not written as something discarded before reaching trust. Reassurance and dependence and trust can be written as two, but may not be separable. Whether trust always comes from accumulation is not decided on this page.

On the other hand, the same word "reassurance" is also sought from a different need. Maslow wrote of the need for safety as a need for a predictable, orderly, reliable world (1943). It includes preferring the familiar and the known, and looking for someone to protect one or a strong person to rely on. When, in the psychological safety section of the part on development, pjdhiro saw reassurance and safety sought in a spirit of self-protection or opportunism as a state like dependence through psychological defense mechanisms (the fold in the chapter "[Where are the words of business misunderstood?](#ldd-words)"), it was reassurance of this side. Both kinds of reassurance are expressions of people always relying on something somewhere, and cannot be sorted into good and bad. Set side by side, the two kinds of reassurance look different in what they seek protection from.
