<details><summary>More — how certain are intention, control and consciousness?</summary>

The word "business development" suggests the act of intentionally starting a business and moving it along as one wishes. Let us look at the three in turn from the side of research.

- **Intention**: Mintzberg and Waters (1983) distinguished strategies that were intended and realized, strategies that were intended but not realized, and strategies that took shape without being intended (emergent strategies). Mintzberg also wrote in 1994 that every strategy has some emergent quality.
- **Control**: Knight (1921) distinguished "risk", which can be estimated by probability, from "uncertainty", where even the probability is unknown. And Knight thought that business profit arises from taking on this uncertainty.
- **Consciousness**: Weick et al. (1999) wrote that people, after starting to act, use the results as clues to find out, looking back, what is happening. pjdhiro sees this as similar to "[dots connect looking backward](#ldd-dots)" in Jobs's speech. The paper by Weick and colleagues also introduces a story Weick has cited again and again. A small Hungarian unit lost in a snowstorm in the Alps calms down when one of them finds a map, and relying on the map, makes its way back. When the lieutenant who had sent them out looks at the map, it is a map of the Pyrenees. Weick reads it as saying that when you are lost, any map will do, as long as it gets you moving. However, this story is a poem written in 1977 by the poet Holub from something told by the Nobel laureate Szent-Györgyi, and whether it actually happened has not been confirmed (Basbøll and Graham, 2006). Wegner (1999, 2003) argued that the very feeling that "I did it" may be an interpretation the mind constructs by inferring from the relation between thought and action.

Lined up like this, "business development" may have the aspect of being a name for what happened, arranged afterward into a single story. At the same time, in Knight's distinction, business is also carried forward while taking on a future whose probabilities are unknown. This page asks about what did not make it into that story, and what was moving before the story. The same shape appears within one person, in a group of people, and in society.

<!--REF:Management studies and entrepreneurship research-->

Sources: Mintzberg & Waters (1983) "The Mind of the Strategist(s)", in S. Srivastva and Associates, *The Executive Mind*, Jossey-Bass, pp.58–83 (full text checked against [a scan of the book on the author's site](https://mintzberg.org/sites/default/files/article/download/hm_the_mind_of_the_strategist.pdf); the definitions of the three strategies p.60) / [Mintzberg (1994)](https://doi.org/10.1016/0024-6301(94)90185-6) "Rethinking Strategic Planning Part I", *Long Range Planning* 27(3):12–21 (full text checked against [the PDF of the journal pages on the author's site](https://mintzberg.org/sites/default/files/article/download/rethinking_strategic_planning_1.pdf); the passage on strategies having an emergent quality p.19) / Knight (1921) *Risk, Uncertainty and Profit* (full text checked against [the public-domain full text in Liberty Fund's Library of Economics and Liberty](https://www.econlib.org/library/Knight/knRUP.html); the distinction between risk and uncertainty in paragraph III.VIII.1 of Part III chapter 8, profit and uncertainty in paragraph III.X.33 of Part III chapter 10) / [Colville, Waterman & Weick (1999)](https://doi.org/10.1177/135050849961006) "Organizing and the Search for Excellence: Making Sense of the Times in Theory and Practice", *Organization* 6(1):129–148 (full text checked against [the Wayback Machine record of the PDF of the journal pages that was in the University of Michigan institutional repository](https://web.archive.org/web/20170820222352/https://deepblue.lib.umich.edu/bitstream/handle/2027.42/68948/10.1177_135050849961006.pdf?sequence=2) (20 August 2017); the passage citing p.55 of Weick 1995 p.141; the story of the unit and the map pp.141–142, citing p.54 of Weick 1995; the reading that any map will do p.142) / [Basbøll & Graham (2006)](https://ephemerajournal.org/sites/default/files/6-2basboell-graham.pdf) "Substitutes for Strategy Research: Notes on the source of Karl Weick's anecdote of the young lieutenant and the map of the Pyrenees", *ephemera* 6(2):194–204 (full text checked against the PDF published by the journal; that the story comes from Szent-Györgyi's telling and Holub's 1977 poem, and that it has never been confirmed as historical fact, p.196) / [Wegner & Wheatley (1999)](https://doi.org/10.1037/0003-066X.54.7.480) "Apparent mental causation", *American Psychologist* 54(7):480 (full text checked against [the Wayback Machine record of the PDF of the journal pages that was on the author's Harvard University page](https://web.archive.org/web/20051215153037/http://www.wjh.harvard.edu:80/%7Ewegner/pdfs/Wegner&Wheatley1999.pdf) (15 December 2005); abstract p.480) / [Wegner (2003)](https://doi.org/10.1016/S1364-6613(03)00002-0) "The mind's best trick", *Trends in Cognitive Sciences* 7(2):65 (full text checked against [the Wayback Machine record of the PDF of the journal pages that was on the same page](https://web.archive.org/web/20051109055505/http://www.wjh.harvard.edu:80/~wegner/pdfs/Mind%27s_Best_Trick.pdf) (9 November 2005); p.65).

</details>

<details><summary>More — "development" in the dictionary, and "development" on this page</summary>

In Japanese dictionaries, *kaihatsu* (development) means clearing forest or wasteland to make fields, making use of natural resources to start industries, devising new technologies or products and putting them to practical use, and drawing out wisdom or abilities. In every sense, it is written as an act in which a person stands as the subject and intentionally changes land, resources or products.

Older usages keep a different direction too. Before the Meiji era it was often read *kaihotsu*, and the *Shōbōgenzō* has the usage of a practitioner who "was opened up (*kaihotsu*) by the cook and had a great awakening". It is a usage in which the person stands on the side of being opened and guided. An encyclopedia also writes that English develop means development as a transitive verb and growth as an intransitive verb.

The "development" of this page places the driving source in what I want to call Love, and places people inside that field. Moving things along intentionally is included both in the dictionary's "development" and in this page's. The difference is whether one sees the part moved along intentionally as the whole of development, or as a part people have been able to take out of the whole.

<!--REF:Words and dictionaries-->

Sources: [*Seisenban Nihon kokugo daijiten*, "開発 (kaihatsu)"](https://kotobank.jp/word/%E9%96%8B%E7%99%BA) senses 1–6 and supplementary note, and the usage example of "開発 (kaihotsu)" (*Shōbōgenzō*) / [*Dejitaru Daijisen*, "開発"](https://kotobank.jp/word/%E9%96%8B%E7%99%BA) senses 1–3 and supplementary explanation / [*Nihon daihyakka zensho (Nipponica)*, "開発"](https://kotobank.jp/word/%E9%96%8B%E7%99%BA), the passage on develop (all checked against the text on Kotobank, 2026-11-10; in Japanese).

</details>

<a id="ldd-development-scope"></a>The breadth of development as this page uses it is laid out in six: who, what, when, where, why and how.

<div class="tablefit cols-2">

| Question | Development as this page uses it |
|---|---|
| What | What is moved along intentionally. What grows and changes without intention |
| Who | One person, a group of people, or society. Even the people making something sometimes cannot tell whether they are making it or it is arising from what surrounds them |
| When | Neither its beginning nor its end is settled as one. Since before the vision is put into words. Beneath envisioning, planning and executing while they go on. Also toward what will likely happen and what may happen |
| Where | Not limited to business. In the projects of life and family too |
| Why | The question is the driving source. The first half answered that it may be what I want to call Love ([That is the driving source of projects](#ldd-conclusion)) |
| How | It proceeds by procedures that can be written in words and numbers. It also proceeds by what one does unconsciously, what is tacit, what is irrational, by feelings and motivation, and by readiness of mind. Force majeure and events not well understood also pile up. It also proceeds by dealing with what cannot be known ahead (dealing with uncertainty) |

</div>

<details><summary>More — what grows without intention is also included in development</summary>

The word development is easily taken, as in business development or product development, as the act of people intentionally making something and moving it along as they wish. Moving things along intentionally certainly is part of development. But as seen at the head of this chapter, intention, control and consciousness all seem less certain than the words suggest. pjdhiro includes in development what is developed unconsciously without intention, what grows, and what changes.

After reading this section, pjdhiro posed this question.

<a id="ldd-grow-age"></a><strong>pjdhiro's view (translated by Claude):</strong>To begin with, have we been able to grow intentionally? All the time since we were born. Even as adults, we learn from raising children, learn from customers' complaints, and keep growing without intending to, and then age without wanting to.

The body receives signals, feelings move, relationships between people change, and the field fluctuates. pjdhiro sees force majeure and events not well understood piling up there too. In the fold "A structure, a viewpoint, a principle, or a process?" in the chapter "[What does Love-Driven Development see as the driving source?](#ldd-what)", it was written that something invisible keeps moving beneath the vision even after it is put into words.

</details>

<details><summary>More — when does development begin, and when does it end?</summary>

The sequence of envisioning, planning and executing can be given a beginning and an end. Setting deadlines and drawing divisions lets one check progress. But those divisions are cut out by people from what is happening and attached to it. Processes not written in the sequence begin before the divisions and continue after them.

The beginning is in most cases seen afterward. As seen in the chapter "[What drives a project?](#ldd-question)", dots may connect looking back, as if preparation within one person had already begun. The field may fluctuate first, and one may start moving riding it.

The end is not settled as one either. "What Is Creation" places the bundle as the completion in which things are integrated as a new form. pjdhiro sees the bundle as having both aspects: completion or deliverables, and, as a direction or gathering, supporting the next field. pjdhiro also sees that a collision may not reach binding: one side may win, or things may return to the field and wait for the next opposition.

Coming undone, drifting apart and going back are also included in development on this page.

<strong>pjdhiro's view (translated by Claude):</strong>The image is like a depth-first search algorithm. I think there is something like exhaustive search that assumes misses. It isn't perfect. But it is reflected in later decisions. If you bundle it in language, it gets called "experience" or "failure". In the sense of a resource needed for development.

Depth-first search is a way of searching that goes down one path to a dead end, and if it is a dead end, goes back and searches the next path.

</details>

<details><summary>More — procedures, and the stance toward vision and planning</summary>

The same goes for the word procedure. pjdhiro includes in the definition of procedure what one does unconsciously. pjdhiro wants to include in the process what is tacit, what is irrational, and feelings and motivation, and to include in procedure even things that cannot be quantified, such as readiness of mind.

About vision and planning, pjdhiro holds the stance that vision and planning may be meaningless, may even be counterproductive in some cases, may be unnecessary in some cases, and necessary in others. pjdhiro even holds the viewpoint that vision and planning are only what human emotions want.

For this stance, pjdhiro named one word to apply.

<a id="ldd-taisho"></a><strong>pjdhiro's view (translated by Claude):</strong>On this, I think the word *taisho* (dealing with things) might be good. For now.

Dealing with things is not directed only at what has already happened. pjdhiro sees envisioning as something done including not only what is happening but also what will likely happen and what may happen, and calls dealing with what has not yet happened dealing with uncertainty.

</details>

<details class="pd-column"><summary>In the projects of life and family too</summary>

This is not limited to business. In the projects of life and family too, within one person, in a group of people and in society, processes not written in the sequence of vision, planning and execution are proceeding. pjdhiro's work over this year can also be read as one example. Within work begun as analysis, it was the one writing who changed (see also: "the app called me" in [How I came to this question — background and history](#ldd-background)).

</details>

<details><summary>More — intended strategy, and strategy realized without being intended</summary>

The view that looks at both what is moved along intentionally and what takes shape without intention also exists in strategy research. In a 1983 chapter, Mintzberg and Waters gave four definitions of strategy. Two of them are strategy as a plan set out in advance, and strategy as a pattern seen in a stream of an organization's decisions and actions. Strategy as pattern, they write, is a definition concerned not with intention but with what was realized, that is, with what actually happened in the organization.

Combining these two definitions, they drew three strategies in one diagram: strategy intended and realized as intended; strategy intended but not realized; and strategy realized as a pattern without being particularly intended (emergent strategy).

What they followed were the strategies of organizations such as companies, newspapers, universities and government agencies. The range pjdhiro points to with the word development is not limited to organizations; it extends to change within one person, to families, and to the movements of society.

<!--REF:Management studies and entrepreneurship research-->

Sources: Mintzberg & Waters (1983) "The Mind of the Strategist(s)", in S. Srivastva and Associates, *The Executive Mind*, Jossey-Bass, pp.58–83 (full text checked against [a scan of the book on the author's site](https://mintzberg.org/sites/default/files/article/download/hm_the_mind_of_the_strategist.pdf); the four definitions p.59, the diagram and definitions of the three strategies p.60, the organizations studied p.61).

</details>

<a id="ldd-development-relation"></a>Then how does development relate to projects and creation?

On this page, the three can be read as names that touch one event from different sides. In the first question, projects are asked about alongside living, and include not only business but life and family. Creation is the shape in which things come into being, proceeding field → wave → edge → vortex → bundle. Development is that shape proceeding, driven by the driving source. Each name is right, and none is the whole, so this page does not fix the relation among the three as one.

<details><summary>More — the relation of the three as generally put, and how this page reads it</summary>

In Japanese dictionaries, a project is "a development plan for research, business and the like", and also "a plan; a planned undertaking; a research and development plan". In the field of project management, the outline is clearer. A public document of the Project Management Association of Japan, citing the definition in a 1985 glossary, writes of project work as non-routine, non-repetitive, one-time work to achieve a specific goal within certain limits of time, money and quality, and continues that, to complete that work as initially targeted, one plans, organizes, coordinates and controls. Creation, in the dictionary, is "making something new for the first time by one's own ideas or skills" and "God making the universe and all things" (all translated by Claude).

Generally, the three are placed as if nested. A project is the frame of a plan with a beginning, an end and a goal. Development is the act of putting something new to practical use within it, and creation is bringing something new into being for the first time. Each is spoken of as an act of an intending subject (a person, or God).

After reading this section, pjdhiro pointed out that the image of the word plan cannot be shared.

<a id="ldd-plan-word"></a><strong>pjdhiro's view (translated by Claude):</strong>Whether "a plan with a beginning, an end and a goal" refers to the ideal when tackling something for the first time, or to a routine one has done for years — I think the term plan has something psychological in it. Wanting to control the environment, wanting to put responsibility somewhere, wanting agreement. What everyone was faintly aware of, PMBOK 7 brought in line with reality as predictive and adaptive. There must have been people for whom that was inconvenient. To give an analogy, in the project called life, how does each of us think about the significance of making plans? Some of us think they're unreliable, and for others, wishing it would be so becomes energy.

On this page, people are both subjects of action and one vortex swirling within events. The divisions differ too. A project in general is one-time and aims at completion as initially targeted. In this page's table, neither the beginning nor the end of development is settled as one. In the five stages of "[What Is Creation](https://uminomae.github.io/project-design-theory/cs/)", the bundle becomes the next field again, and may return to the field without reaching integration. What Is Creation writes the five stages as the process by which beings come into being, not limited to human ideas or skills but including matter, phenomena and the mind.

<!--REF:Words and dictionaries-->

Sources: (in Japanese) *Seisenban Nihon kokugo daijiten* and *Dejitaru Daijisen*, ["プロジェクト (project)"](https://kotobank.jp/word/%E3%81%B7%E3%82%8D%E3%81%98%E3%81%88%E3%81%8F%E3%81%A8-3218152) and ["創造 (creation)"](https://kotobank.jp/word/%E5%89%B5%E9%80%A0) (checked against the text on Kotobank, 2026-11-10) / Hiroyuki Hazama, "PM resource guide: project management" (Project Management Association of Japan, Rev.0, 11 March 2001; full text checked against [the public PDF on the association's site](https://pmaj.or.jp/archives/JPMF/library/open/education/PMGuide-01pm-1.pdf); the passage citing the definition in the Engineering Advancement Association of Japan's *Glossary of Engineering Project Management*, 1985).

</details>

<a id="ldd-development-name"></a>A name of the form "X-driven development" is usually the name of a development method, such as test-driven development. Then is Love-Driven Development also the name of a way of doing things?

The name Love-Driven Development was given afterward to events encountered again and again ([background and history](#ldd-name)). The driving source is what I want to call Love, and people are inside it. On whether people place it first, pjdhiro says this.

<strong>pjdhiro's view (translated by Claude):</strong>You can't consciously, actively place it first, but sometimes it has already been placed first unconsciously, and from others' point of view, that is the person's own doing.

Procedures are written on the premise that the person moving things can manipulate the other, so Love-Driven Development does not take the form of a procedure. Even so, people do not do nothing. Where not everything can be controlled, people deal with what is happening and with what may happen. What this page writes is the structure and process of that phenomenon. To this question, pjdhiro answers that Love-Driven Development is "both an analysis of the phenomenon and a way of dealing with it" (on the word dealing, see the fold "[Procedures, and the stance toward vision and planning](#ldd-taisho)").

<details><summary>More — names of methods, and the name Love-Driven Development</summary>

Test-driven development is a way of working in which test code is written before the program itself, and the program code is written so as to pass those tests. A method is something people choose, use following a procedure, and use to come closer to the result they want. The "driven" in the name can be read as saying what the developer places first and proceeds from.

About this reading, pjdhiro says this of the "driven" in Love-Driven Development.

<a id="ldd-drive-moon"></a><strong>pjdhiro's view (translated by Claude):</strong>The "driven" in Love-Driven Development does speak of what people place first. It's just that it can't be shown in symbols. It points to what lies ahead of people. All along. To the moon.

(See also: [the finger pointing at the moon](#ldd-finger))

<!--REF:Words and dictionaries-->

Sources: [IT glossary e-Words, "Test-driven development"](https://e-words.jp/w/%E3%83%86%E3%82%B9%E3%83%88%E9%A7%86%E5%8B%95%E9%96%8B%E7%99%BA.html) (in Japanese; checked against the text, 2026-11-10).

</details>

<details><summary>More — Wilber's integral theory, and the process of development</summary>

After reading this reader, pjdhiro named the philosopher Wilber's integral theory.

<a id="ldd-wilber"></a><strong>pjdhiro's view (translated by Claude):</strong>I think Wilber's integral theory is close too. The process of development. I don't remember the driving source

In a 1997 paper, Wilber looked at consciousness and its development from four aspects: the inside of one person (intention), the outside of one person (behavior), the inside of a group (culture), and the outside of a group (society). Every aspect, Wilber writes, unfolds through a dozen or so stages. And he wrote that inner consciousness can continue a process of transcending while including the earlier stages.

In excerpts from a book that he later put on the author's site, the same view is written moment by moment. The whole of the previous moment is handed to the present moment as something given. It exists before the present moment registers it. But it does not stay merely given. The present moment takes it up, includes and transcends it, and reworks it.

Two places look close. One is pjdhiro's view in this chapter that "sometimes it has already been placed first unconsciously", alongside the point that the previous moment is given before one is aware of it. The other is that the inside and outside of one person and of a group unfold at the same time, which overlaps with "Who" in this chapter's table (one person, a group of people, or society).

Wilber also writes of something corresponding to the driving source. In the excerpts, he gives it the name Eros, and restates it as a "tilt" or a "pull" toward greater depth, complexity, consciousness and care. Alongside Eros, which reaches up, he also writes of Agape, which embraces what came before and reaches down. The way of speaking of a tilt or a pull seems close in the direction of its image to this reader's image of gravity ([Isn't Love like gravity?](#ldd-well)). The pairing of Eros and Agape also sits next to the chapter on [Peirce's agapasm](#ldd-agapasm).

There are also places that look different. Wilber gives Eros a direction toward greater depth, and lines up all the aspects and stages in one frame. Development in this chapter searches assuming misses, includes going back, and does not place its direction as one.

After reading this section, pjdhiro restated Eros and Agape in pjdhiro's own words.

<a id="ldd-wilber-eros-agape"></a><strong>pjdhiro's view (translated by Claude):</strong>A feeling of seeking, and a feeling of embracing, right. A positive activeness, and an active passivity like acceptance. I treat both of those driving sources, while they stay vague, as what I want to call Love.

Wilber wrote what reaches up and what embraces what came before and reaches down separately, under the two names Eros and Agape. This reader does not separate the two, and keeps them under one name. If only the upward direction is seen as the driving source, the movement of the embracing side is placed outside the driving. If both are put into what I want to call Love, both seeking and moving forward and accepting can be read as movements within the same driving.

<!--REF:Psychology-->

Sources: Wilber, K. (1997) "An Integral Theory of Consciousness", *Journal of Consciousness Studies* 4(1):71–92 (full text checked against [the Wayback Machine record](https://web.archive.org/web/2004/http://www.imprint.co.uk/Wilber.htm) of the full text that was on the site of the journal's publisher, Imprint Academic; the four aspects and the stages in the abstract, the process of transcending and including in the section "The Further Reaches of Human Nature") / Wilber, K., excerpt A "An Integral Age at the Leading Edge" and excerpt B "The Many Ways We Touch" from Volume 2 of the Kosmos trilogy (full text checked against the Wayback Machine records of the excerpts the author put on "Ken Wilber Online", the author's site at the publisher Shambhala; the previous moment being given and reworked in [excerpt A, part 4](https://web.archive.org/web/2006/http://wilber.shambhala.com/html/books/kosmos/excerptA/part4-1.cfm), the tilt of Eros in [excerpt A, part 2](https://web.archive.org/web/2006/http://wilber.shambhala.com/html/books/kosmos/excerptA/part2.cfm), the pull in [excerpt A, part 3](https://web.archive.org/web/2006/http://wilber.shambhala.com/html/books/kosmos/excerptA/part3-2.cfm), Eros and Agape in [excerpt B, part 2](https://web.archive.org/web/2006/http://wilber.shambhala.com/html/books/kosmos/excerptB/part2.cfm)).

</details>

## How can the words of development be checked with this view?

<!--OPEN-->

Then how should the words used within development be checked?

<a id="ldd-screen"></a>In pjdhiro's view, vision, the words of the mind, and value are all names given to vortices born from the field, and vortices cannot be cut off from the field. It can be read that misunderstanding arises where they are cut off and handled like parts inside people or products. So in this part, we check the words used within development before applying theories or instructions. pjdhiro sees Love-Driven Development as something that arranges the words and numbers received before thinking and deciding (the input), a sieve that asks whether one is treating vortices as vortices. In this sieve, one first checks whether words such as value, trust, vision and creation are being treated as single things cut off from the field and the waves.

The three chapters that follow look at this in turn: when checking the words one has received (Where are the words of business misunderstood?), when giving something form (Where does design come in?), and what changes when one checks and how far it works (What changes when the input is arranged?). The first chapter deals with words used in business and management settings, so it limits the discussion to business. The other two take examples from business, but the discussion is not limited to business. What this view is good for and in what situations it is useful are looked at together in the Part 7 chapter "[What is this view good for, and in what situations is it useful?](#ldd-use)".


## Where are the words of business misunderstood?

<!--OPEN-->

The use of checking the words one has received is looked at with three words often used in business settings: vision, the words of the mind, and value creation.

### Vision

Where does vision come from?

The order often shown as the way to run a business is to first research the market and draw up a vision, and then start moving. There is research that looked at how experienced entrepreneurs proceed. Dew, Sarasvathy and colleagues gave 27 entrepreneurs, who had founded several companies and had more than 15 years of experience, and 37 students at a business school (MBA), the same task of starting a business with a new product, and asked them to think aloud. Many of the entrepreneurs rejected methods of market research such as questionnaires. Those who said they did not believe the market forecast figures written in the task were 15 of the 27 entrepreneurs and 2 of the 37 students. One entrepreneur said that instead of researching, he would first go and sell, even before the machine existed.

The view this study rests on is called effectuation (it is also placed in the table of "[What has management studies seen as the driving source?](#ldd-management)"). It is the view of starting from the means at hand and producing new ends. Dew and colleagues compare it to the difference between a cook who makes a dish by following a recipe and a cook who imagines what dishes can be made from the ingredients at hand, and see the thinking of experienced entrepreneurs as the latter form.

pjdhiro says something close to this form when introducing himself.

<a id="ldd-housekeeper"></a><strong>pjdhiro's experience (translated by Claude):</strong>When I introduce myself, I often say that I'm good at the kind of things a housekeeper does. I think there is an image of people who lead projects as strong people who have a vision of the future, have a conception and a plan for getting there, and have forecasts and convictions that pierce through uncertainty, and I'm not that kind of person, so please don't misunderstand. Against uncertainty, it may be a simple process of just doing (going to the site + judging and adapting) recursively.

A housekeeper puts together what can be done from the ingredients and tools in the house they visit and from how life is going there that day. This can be read as the work of the cook who imagines what dishes can be made from the ingredients at hand. The way of proceeding by repeatedly going to the site, judging and adapting also overlaps with what one entrepreneur in the study by Dew and colleagues said: instead of researching, first go and sell. It is one aspect: a vision may also take shape at the end of moving. About where vision comes from, pjdhiro also lines up other aspects.

<a id="ldd-vision"></a><strong>pjdhiro's view (translated by Claude):</strong>Vision may be something wonderful like wisdom, may be mere psychological defense, may be born at the end of action.

Where, why and how vision arises is still not well understood (the chapter "[What drives a project?](#ldd-question)"). pjdhiro sees that until the driving source working before it, what I want to call Love, is understood, what vision is may not be understood either. If one fixes its source as one thing while the 5W1H, structure and process of what is taken as vision are not understood, one thinks one understands, and the searching stops. In the terms of the chapter "[How do expectation and trust differ?](#ldd-expect)", expectation rides on the image of the future that a vision holds. This may be where vision moves people. Whether that image foresees what lies ahead or is maintained to protect oneself from anxiety is hard to tell apart from the content of the image alone.

pjdhiro's view on the word business conception being called disappointing is in the chapter "[How do expectation and trust differ?](#ldd-uncertain-practice)".

<details class="pd-column"><summary>If we had called it understood with the atom</summary>

When the atom was found, the explanation that matter is made of atoms was established. But if we had called it understood there, the nucleus inside the atom, and the still smaller elementary particles, would not have been looked for.

</details>

<details><summary>More — is vision psychological defense? (an image carrying expectation, and clues for telling apart)</summary>

pjdhiro sees whether something ends in disappointment as depending on ability and on the strength of psychological defense mechanisms. Following this view, if ability catches up with the image, the image becomes reality. If defense is strong, it can be read that one either keeps the image without admitting the betrayal (idealization), or stops moving just before being betrayed.

That uncertainty about future threat makes preparation hard, and that this leads to anxiety (Grupe & Nitschke, 2013), was seen in the fold of the chapter "[What is anxiety?](#ldd-uncertain)". When one feels the unseen ahead as a threat and the signal of survival is welling up, a vision may be easily sought as a picture that fills what lies ahead. It looks like the same shape as the misapplication of trying to solve a problem of trust by achieving survival.

Clues for telling apart come from the difference between expectation and trust.

- In pjdhiro's view, expectation is not something that sharpens estimates. If so, estimating is done with another tool. Kahneman and Tversky (1977) separated the view from the content of the plan at hand from the view from the distribution of outcomes of similar cases, and wrote that relying on the former tends to make plans optimistic. Whether the vision stays as it is when set beside the numbers of similar cases is one clue.
- Whether the image is made only of good things, or includes what does not fit. In the terms of the chapter "When signals and thoughts part, one feels a lack", a vision that keeps what does not fit held as a kesson can be read as holding good and bad together. Whether it can be called trust is not decided here, since what trust is is still being searched for. Idealization keeps the image by splitting off what does not fit.

If expectation is the energy to start moving, then a vision working as defense may also keep moving people. Taylor and Brown (1988), summarizing research, argued that the tendency to see oneself and the future as better than they are is a feature of ordinary thinking, and seems to support other aspects of mental health. There is also the aspect that without expectation one could not have started. In the sequence of recovery from social withdrawal that pjdhiro cites (the chapter "[How do expectation and trust differ?](#ldd-expect)"), expectation and conceit are also placed first. The person telling them apart also stands in the same uncertainty. So telling apart is not done once; it is repeated while moving.

<!--REF:Psychology-->

Sources: Kahneman & Tversky (1977) *Intuitive Prediction: Biases and Corrective Procedures*, Decision Research Technical Report PTR-1042-77-6 (a technical report commissioned by the US Defense Advanced Research Projects Agency; full text checked against [the Wayback Machine record of the DTIC ADA047747 PDF](https://web.archive.org/web/20150204120725/http://www.dtic.mil/dtic/tr/fulltext/u2/a047747.pdf) (4 February 2015); p.2-2) / [Taylor & Brown (1988)](https://doi.org/10.1037/0033-2909.103.2.193) *Psychological Bulletin* 103(2):193 (full text checked against [the PDF of the journal pages on the site of author Taylor's UCLA lab](https://taylorlab.psych.ucla.edu/wp-content/uploads/sites/5/2014/10/1988_Illusion-and-well-being_A-social-psychological-perspective-on-mental-health.pdf); abstract on p.193).

</details>

<details><summary>Sources</summary>

The study of the task given to entrepreneurs and MBA students: [Dew, Read, Sarasvathy & Wiltbank (2009)](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jbusvent.2008.02.002) "Effectual versus predictive logics in entrepreneurial decision-making: Differences between experts and novices", *Journal of Business Venturing* 24(4):287–309 (full text checked against [the PDF of the journal pages](https://effectuation.org/hubfs/Journal%20Articles/Other%20Unfiled%20Articles/Effectual-versus-predictive-logics-copy-1.pdf) placed on effectuation.org, the base of the authors' research; the 27 and 37 people in the abstract on p.287, how the entrepreneurs were chosen p.288, the rejection of market research and the numbers of those who did not believe the forecast figures p.298, the entrepreneur's words about first going to sell in Table 5 on p.298, calling the entrepreneurs' thinking effectual p.288, the logic of starting from means to produce new ends and the comparison with cooks p.293).

</details>

### The words of the mind

Then where else, besides vision, can this view be used?

When Google's people operations department studied more than 180 teams inside the company, the people doing the analysis expected to find the combination of individual traits and skills that makes an excellent team. The result was different: how members interact with one another mattered more than who is on the team, and what stood out far above the rest was psychological safety. That term, psychological safety, is now sometimes received in workplaces as if it were a quality inside each individual. It can be read that a way of receiving that sees the goodness of a team as a combination of what lies inside each individual shows both in the researchers' initial expectation and in how the term came to be received once it spread.

<a id="ldd-words"></a><strong>pjdhiro's view (translated by Claude):</strong>I feel that the misunderstandings likely to arise around management buzzwords about the mind could be explained by differences in viewpoint. I think that is a use of Love-Driven Development.

Words about the mind, such as psychological safety, GRIT, intrinsic motivation and negative capability, are often used in management settings. Many are written on the theory side as matters of groups and relationships, yet in the field they tend to be received as abilities, qualities or states inside the person, things to be cultivated, measured or drawn out. From the side this page has been looking from, reassurance, perseverance and motivation are all names given to vortices that field and fluctuation produce, with feet both inside people and between people. Each word works as it is in situations that suit it, and misunderstanding arises at differences in viewpoint.

<details><summary>More — word by word (definition, common misunderstandings, interpretation from the viewpoint of Love-Driven Development, conditions under which it works)</summary>

##### Psychological safety

<div class="tablefit cols-2">

| Definition | A shared belief held by members of a team that the team is safe for interpersonal risk taking. It rests on trust and respect, and points to a climate in which people are comfortable being themselves (Edmondson, 1999). Schein writes of it as a condition that lowers learning anxiety so that disconfirming information can be received (Schein, 1995) |
|---|---|
| Common misunderstandings | Getting along well; having no strictness. A system in which one is not punished. Something that can be made by declaring "please say anything" and putting systems in place. Something that is better the higher it is, in any organization |
| Interpretation from the viewpoint of Love-Driven Development | The source encompasses both not having to fear punishment or ridicule, and being trusted and respected. In the field, only the former, which is easy to confirm with mechanisms and systems, is sometimes used. In the words of pjdhiro's question: hasn't it turned, before we knew it, into something with the nuance of caring only for lower-order needs? |
| Conditions under which it works | pjdhiro sees it as perhaps working in work that deals with intangible things, such as services and design |

</div>

Then, if one is psychologically safe and reassured, do good things happen?

<strong>pjdhiro's view (translated by Claude):</strong>No. Reassurance and safety sought in a spirit of self-protection or opportunism can also be a state like dependence through psychological defense mechanisms.

The reassurance Schein wrote of was a condition for being able to receive disconfirming information. When reassurance of the same name is sought as a condition for continuing to be protected from that information, it works on the side of keeping defense up rather than letting it down (see also: [The mind has mechanisms to protect itself](#ldd-defense)).

To read the theory of psychological safety, you can go from [What Is Psychological Safety](psychological-safety-en.html) to the papers of Edmondson and Schein.

##### GRIT

<div class="tablefit cols-2">

| Definition | Passion and perseverance for long-term goals. Maintaining effort and interest over years despite failure, adversity and plateaus (Duckworth et al., 2007) |
|---|---|
| Common misunderstandings | Endurance or guts. The strength to grit one's teeth and bear it. What people who quit did not have |
| Interpretation from the viewpoint of Love-Driven Development | It can be read that one can keep persevering because one is drawn to a center and orbits around it. Measuring shows only the perseverance; what one was drawn by does not show up in the numbers |
| Conditions under which it works | When describing, afterward, the behavior of people who keep going because they are drawn |

</div>

In reading GRIT, what pjdhiro is concerned with first is what kind of endurance the perseverance at its core points to.

<strong>pjdhiro's view (translated by Claude):</strong>GRIT is perseverance, isn't it. The thing called endurance hasn't been broken down much. I think it's close to wabi. Resignation and acceptance.

The definition above says maintaining effort and interest over years despite failure, adversity and plateaus. Within the same behavior of maintaining, different states of mind can be present. There is a hard endurance that pushes back with gritted teeth, and there is also a soft endurance that keeps going while accepting that things do not go as one wishes. Where it overlaps with the process of recovering after adversity (the [resilience](#ldd-resilience) entry below) cannot be told from the definition either. If one measures perseverance and tries to cultivate it without separating what workings of the mind support that endurance, it may easily drift toward "endurance or guts" in the common misunderstandings row. The resignation and acceptance pjdhiro named in the card are the states of mind of the soft side of endurance.

<a id="ldd-wabi"></a>pjdhiro describes that soft endurance with the image of wabi.

<strong>pjdhiro's view (translated by Claude):</strong>For me, wabi has the image of endurance beyond resignation.

According to dictionaries and encyclopedias, the word *wabi* originally pointed to being dispirited and to worrying. In the life of medieval grass hermitages and the tea ceremony, it also came to be valued positively as a word for the quiet, lonely taste found in simplicity. The Edo-period tea book *Zencharoku*, quoted by the encyclopedia, writes that wabi is not giving rise to the thought of being constrained even when constrained, and not raising the thought of lack even when lacking (the fold below). This can be read as a form of endurance that, in a different direction from pushing back against lack and bearing it, receives the lack as it is and carries on. Bearing not knowing without hurrying to fill it is also treated in the chapter "[What is anxiety?](#ldd-uncertain)" and in the entry on [negative capability](#ldd-negative-capability) below.

<details><summary>More — how wabi is written in dictionaries and encyclopedias</summary>

A Japanese dictionary treats *wabi* as the noun form of the verb *wabiru* and lists four senses. The first is feeling forlorn, worrying, being dispirited; the example given is a poem from the *Man'yōshū*. The second is enjoying a quiet retired life. The third is the quiet, tranquil taste spoken of in the tea ceremony and haikai: a calm, lonely feeling within simplicity. An example is attached from an early Edo collection of anecdotes, that Sen no Rikyū, as "the true meaning of wabi", always hummed the poem "To those who wait only for the flowers, I would show the spring of the grass between the snow in a mountain village" (translated by Claude). The fourth, written with the character 詫, is apologizing.

An encyclopedia takes *wabi* as the noun form of the verb *wabu*, and writes that it originally meant falling into misfortune and decline, being disappointed and dejected, and as a result feeling helpless and transient. It continues that in the thatched-hermitage life of medieval recluses this came to be highly valued as proof of freedom from a view that prizes worldly honor and interest, and further became the fundamental spirit of the "wabi-suki" tea of Takeno Jōō, Sen no Rikyū and others. The same entry quotes the following passage from the Edo-period tea book *Zencharoku*.

> 其不自由なるも，不自由なりとおもふ念を不生，不足も不足の念を起さず，不調も不調の念を抱かぬを侘なりと心得べきなり

It means: one should understand wabi as not giving rise to the thought of being constrained even when constrained, not raising the thought of lack even when lacking, and not harboring the thought of disorder even when things are not in order (translated by Claude).

<!--REF:Eastern thought-->

Sources: (in Japanese) *Seisenban Nihon kokugo daijiten*, ["わび【侘・詫】"](https://kotobank.jp/word/%E4%BE%98%E3%81%B3) (Shogakukan; full text checked against the public version on Kotobank) / *Kaitei shinban Sekai daihyakka jiten*, ["侘"](https://kotobank.jp/word/%E4%BE%98-152618) (Heibonsha; the section written by Nobuo Hori; full text checked against the public version on Kotobank; the passage from *Zencharoku* is as quoted in this entry).

</details>

<a id="ldd-resilience"></a>

##### Resilience

<div class="tablefit cols-2">

| Definition | A process in which positive adaptation occurs within significant adversity (Luthar et al., 2000) |
|---|---|
| Common misunderstandings | An unbreakable mind. Individual strength acquired by training. What people who could not recover did not have |
| Interpretation from the viewpoint of Love-Driven Development | In research, resilience is written as the flow of recovery that occurs after adversity, and occurs through both the person's own strength and the support around them. In workplaces, only the aspect of strength the person has tends to be taken out of it. In pjdhiro's view, recovery is close to a process of self-renewal in which a new self is created. Its key lies in self-acceptance of a kind that removes psychological defense mechanisms. Fluctuations and inner oppositions born in adversity swirl into a vortex, and from there the next self takes shape. The same shape as fluctuations opposing within one person and conflict producing something, seen in the value creation section of the chapter "[Where are the words of business misunderstood?](#ldd-creation)" |
| Conditions under which it works | When looking, after adversity, at both the person and the field that supports them |

</div>

<strong>pjdhiro's view (translated by Claude):</strong>…Perseverance is resignation and acceptance, and resilience too is, I think, a stage of development, or rather a state, produced by a state with few psychological defense mechanisms in the same way.

<a id="ldd-intrinsic"></a>

##### Intrinsic motivation

<div class="tablefit cols-2">

| Definition | Doing an activity for the inherent interest and satisfaction of the activity itself (Ryan and Deci, 2000). The theory depicts this, as a tendency to seek novelty and challenge, to extend one's capacities and to learn, as an expression of the positive potential of human nature |
|---|---|
| Common misunderstandings | A psychological state that is in everyone and works the same way if drawn out. A good thing to be cultivated. Something raised by rewards and goals |
| Interpretation from the viewpoint of Love-Driven Development | In pjdhiro's view, the misunderstanding lies in speaking of one aspect of an ideal state as a psychological state. pjdhiro sees the stages of adult development theory as the only thing that can express it well (the fold under the table) |
| Conditions under which it works | Activities attractive for their novelty, challenge or beauty. There, choice and having one's feelings acknowledged strengthen motivation, while rewards contingent on performance, deadlines and imposed goals weaken it (Ryan and Deci, 2000) |

</div>

Then where does intrinsic motivation well up from? The theory does not ask that; it treats it as a tendency acquired in evolution, and studies the conditions that draw it out and sustain it and the conditions that weaken it. This reader has looked one step further there. The view is that people, within the distortion of the field we want to call Love, feel in the body the gap between Love and me as a lack, and are moved by that lack (the chapter "[That is the driving source of projects](#ldd-conclusion)"). In this view, being moved by the interest of the activity itself is also a name given to one of the vortices that this driving produces. pjdhiro sees the same driving as drifting toward results, toward money, fame or power, or toward things that do not turn into money, such as knowledge ([the viewpoint added on the entrepreneur's mind](#ldd-drift)). The misunderstanding of intrinsic motivation can be read as lying in taking out from all of this only the ideal direction of growing and learning.

<details><summary>More — one aspect of an ideal state, and a psychological state</summary>

The "Interpretation from the viewpoint of Love-Driven Development" row of the table was written from the following words of pjdhiro's, said while talking about where intrinsic motivation is misunderstood.

<strong>pjdhiro's view (translated by Claude):</strong>The misunderstanding of "intrinsic motivation" is that it speaks of one aspect of an "ideal state" — as a psychological state. But I think the only thing that can express that well is the stages of adult development theory.

Ryan and Deci (2000) depict intrinsic motivation as an expression of the positive potential of human nature, seeking novelty and challenge and wanting to learn. pjdhiro sees this as one aspect of a certain ideal state. In workplaces, it is received as a psychological state that is in everyone and works the same way if drawn out (the "Common misunderstandings" row of the table). What pjdhiro calls a misunderstanding is this mix-up.

What pjdhiro names as expressing the ideal state is the stages of adult development theory. From stage to stage, what looks most threatening and how one protects the mind against it change (the chapter "[What looks threatening changes with the stage of development](#ldd-stages)").

The theory also describes ways of moving that, even though driven from within, are not felt as part of oneself. Ryan and Deci called acting to avoid guilt or anxiety, or to attain ego enhancements such as pride, introjection. The mind's moves to protect itself from anxiety were seen in the chapter "[The mind has mechanisms to protect itself](#ldd-defense)". About psychological defense mechanisms and intrinsic motivation, pjdhiro says the following.

<strong>pjdhiro's view (translated by Claude):</strong>People end up feeling along their psychological defense mechanisms, and even go as far as acting out. That can't be stopped by strong motivation. Like mental illness or personality disorders. So measures taken to "use intrinsic motivation in a convenient direction" are probably close to psychiatry or treatment

<!--REF:Psychology-->

Sources: [Ryan & Deci (2000)](https://doi.org/10.1037/0003-066X.55.1.68) *American Psychologist* 55(1):68 (positive potential pp.69–70, introjection p.73; the full text was checked on the page "[What is intrinsic motivation?](intrinsic-motivation.html)" (in Japanese)). pjdhiro's two remarks are also placed on that page.

</details>

To read the theory of intrinsic motivation, you can go from [What is intrinsic motivation?](intrinsic-motivation.html) (in Japanese) to the paper of Ryan and Deci. The theory of stages can be followed from [What are stages of mental development?](developmental-stages.html) (in Japanese).

<a id="ldd-negative-capability"></a>

##### Negative capability

<div class="tablefit cols-2">

| Definition | Being capable of being in uncertainties, mysteries and doubts without any irritable reaching after fact and reason. The poet Keats named it, in a letter to the poet's brothers in December 1817, as the quality that goes to form a man of achievement, especially in literature, and which Shakespeare possessed so enormously (Keats, 1817). In later research, for example, a paper studying public health nurses in Japanese local governments introduces it as the ability to stay in uncertainty and ambiguity without rushing to conclusions (Ujihara et al., 2026). 🚧 How the word has been used in psychoanalysis and clinical practice is under construction (exploration: [#119](https://github.com/uminomae/project-design-theory/issues/119)) |
|---|---|
| Common misunderstandings | It is taken as the endurance of gritting one's teeth and bearing what has no answer. It is also sometimes taken as indecision that puts off deciding, or as an individual ability to be trained and increased |
| Interpretation from the viewpoint of Love-Driven Development | This passage of Keats's letter has no words of enduring or effort. The poet Coleridge, given as the opposite example, is written of as one who cannot remain content with half-knowledge. What anxiety is is looked at in the chapter "[What is anxiety?](#ldd-uncertain)" and on the separate page "[What is anxiety?](anxiety.html)" (in Japanese). 🚧 How this word relates to the [holding](#ldd-hold) of Kesson-Driven Thinking and to pjdhiro's view in the GRIT entry (endurance as resignation and acceptance) is under construction (exploration: [#119](https://github.com/uminomae/project-design-theory/issues/119)) |
| Conditions under which it works | Keats closes this passage of the letter by saying that, pursued through, it would perhaps take one no further than this: that with a great poet the sense of Beauty overcomes every other consideration, or rather obliterates all consideration (Keats, 1817) |

</div>

<a id="ldd-conflict"></a>
##### Conflict

<div class="tablefit cols-2">

| Definition | Psychology calls a state in which two or more motives or goals compete and one is torn over which to take conflict. Opposition between people over discrepant wishes, opinions or norms is also called interpersonal conflict (*Saishin shinrigaku jiten*). For ways of handling it, there is an arrangement that divides five modes — competing, collaborating, compromising, avoiding and accommodating — on the two axes of cooperativeness and assertiveness (Kilmann and Thomas, 1977) |
|---|---|
| Common misunderstandings | Taking conflict as either good or bad. Either avoiding it or settling it quickly as something bad, or, conversely, seeking it as something that is better the more one clashes. Among researchers and practitioners too, an optimistic view that conflict raises performance and creativity and should be encouraged, and a pessimistic view that it usually harms functioning, have been argued side by side (Greer et al., 2012) |
| Interpretation from the viewpoint of Love-Driven Development | pjdhiro sees conflict as having no good or bad: it may be bad, and it also has an aspect of collision just before creation. pjdhiro also sees much misunderstanding where an opposition of two things is sorted into good and bad by psychological impression (below the table) |
| Conditions under which it works | The review sums up that conflict is less likely to harm functioning when conflicts over the content of the task can be handled apart from conflicts over personality and relationships, and when members feel they are within the same goals. Suppressed conflict grows over time and becomes harder to resolve (Greer et al., 2012) |

</div>

Having read the conflict table, pjdhiro says the following about the good and bad of conflict.

<strong>pjdhiro's view (translated by Claude):</strong>Conflict has no good or bad. While giving examples of bad cases, it also has a positive side, exactly as the collision just before creation. Like "a crisis is a chance!" With double binds and cognitive dissonance and so on, people judge an opposition of two things as good or bad by psychological impression, so I feel there is a lot of misunderstanding.

Research on team conflict does not settle conflict as one of good or bad either. According to the review, conflict over personality and relationships consistently tends to lower performance, and conflict over task procedures and roles tends to lower performance and satisfaction even more. For example, if an anesthesiologist speaks ill of a surgeon's character or competence in the middle of an operation, the tension produced only gets in the way of the work (the review's illustration). On the other hand, conflict over the content of the task showed, in a meta-analysis combining 116 studies, no relation to performance overall, and worked in the direction of raising performance in teams that could handle it apart from relationship conflict (Greer et al. 2012). "[What Is Creation](https://uminomae.github.io/project-design-theory/cs/)" writes the edge of the five stages of creation as "the border where heterogeneous things meet and collide".

<details><summary>More — how do conflict, cognitive dissonance and the double bind differ?</summary>

All three look like two things in opposition. What differs is where, and what collides with what.

- **Conflict**: two or more motives or goals competing within one person. Lewin divided this into three types: wanting both (approach–approach), wanting to avoid both (avoidance–avoidance), and being both drawn to and wanting to avoid one goal (approach–avoidance). Discrepancies of wishes, opinions or norms between people are also called interpersonal conflict (*Saishin shinrigaku jiten*).
- **Cognitive dissonance**: a state within one person in which things one knows or believes do not fit with one another. The misfit produces pressure to reduce it, and people may reduce it by changing their own thoughts or attitudes (*Dejitaru Daijisen*; Festinger and Carlsmith 1959). In Festinger and Carlsmith's experiment, people made to tell others that a boring task had been interesting, and paid one dollar, later themselves rated the task as interesting. Those paid twenty dollars did not change their ratings. It is written that they persuaded themselves to fit what they had said.
- **Double bind**: 🚧 under construction (to be written after reading the original by Bateson et al. 1956; exploration: [#121](https://github.com/uminomae/project-design-theory/issues/121)).

</details>

##### Entrepreneurship

<div class="tablefit cols-2">

| Definition | The mind and actions of the entrepreneur who finds, or creates, opportunities (the "entrepreneur's mind" row of the management studies table) |
|---|---|
| Common misunderstandings | Something to be given to people through education |
| Interpretation from the viewpoint of Love-Driven Development | What moves that mind lies beyond the reach of the table. It connects to the hypothesis in "[Is being itself the source?](#ldd-conclusion-being)" in the chapter "What is still not known", that managers too may be one point orbiting a center |
| Conditions under which it works | As a description of situations in which a vision brings people together |

</div>

<details><summary>Sources</summary>

Google's study of teams: Julia Rozovsky "The five keys to a successful Google team", re:Work (the site of Google's people operations department), 17 November 2015 (full text checked against [the Wayback Machine record](https://web.archive.org/web/20160113235433/https://rework.withgoogle.com/blog/five-keys-to-a-successful-google-team/) (13 January 2016); two years, more than 200 interviews and more than 180 teams; the expectation of finding a combination of individual traits and skills; that how people interact mattered more than who is on the team; the five factors, and psychological safety being by far the most important).
