### Value creation

The saying that business is value creation is everywhere, in management studies and in business practice. Then where is that value born, and who does that "creation"?

In an experiment by Norton, Mochon and Ariely, people who had folded origami while looking at instructions were asked how much they would pay for their own work, and they put a value of 23 cents on average. The value that people who had not folded put on the same works was 5 cents; they saw them as almost scrap paper. The value the folders put on their works was almost the same as the 27 cents other people put on works folded by people skilled at origami. The value of the same paper was born not in the paper but on the side of the person who folded it. As seen in the chapter "[Value is born in the consciousness of the one who receives](#ldd-value)", the state of feeling value is in the consciousness of the one who receives it. In this experiment, the folder was that one who receives. What the maker put in becomes a trigger for signals to well up in the one who receives.

Then who does creation? pjdhiro sees it this way.

<a id="ldd-creation"></a><strong>pjdhiro's view (translated by Claude):</strong>Creation is not only an individual's ability.

If creation is the process in which field and fluctuation produce vortices, vortices cannot be cut off from the field, and the person who makes can also be read as one vortex in the field. pjdhiro sees that even when one person produces something alone, fluctuations may be opposing within that one person, and conflict may be producing something.

<details><summary>More — value creation as a slogan, and two observations</summary>

pjdhiro sees that because the relation between field and vortex is not common sense, the phrase value creation is held up like a slogan and, like many buzzwords, misunderstood. That is the reading that value is something embedded in products, and creation is something creators do.

From the two viewpoints so far, the signals of consciousness and creation, two observations about this reading emerge. Where is value born in the first place? Who does creation in the first place?

pjdhiro also sees that looking only at the vortex called the individual tends to make one take lightly the field that cannot be cut off from that vortex, and the oppositions and fluctuations in it. With that view, one may tend to try to make only the vortex directly, without engaging with the field. This is the same whether giving instructions to people or making something oneself.

</details>

<details><summary>More — profit, and the question of the long term</summary>

pjdhiro says that understanding Love-Driven Development lets one see that what is held to be good is a conditional right answer. The question given as an example is the following.

<a id="ldd-profit"></a><strong>pjdhiro's question (translated by Claude):</strong>Suppose "making a profit is good" — even at the cost of killing the long term?

Deciding by the yardstick of profit alone may destroy the very shape of the field that was drawing people. pjdhiro sees Love-Driven Development as bringing the way of thinking and viewpoint needed when falling into this dilemma, and that as an element needed for managers' decision-making.

</details>

<details><summary>Sources</summary>

The origami experiment: [Norton, Mochon & Ariely (2012)](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jcps.2011.08.002) "The IKEA effect: When labor leads to love", *Journal of Consumer Psychology* 22(3):453–460 (full text checked against [the authors' manuscript in DASH, Harvard University's institutional repository](http://nrs.harvard.edu/urn-3:HUL.InstRepos:12136084); the 23 cents, 5 cents and the 27 cents for skilled folders' works in Experiment 1B are on pp.10–11 of the manuscript).

</details>


## Where does design come in?

<!--OPEN-->

Then how can this view be used on the side of giving something form? If the recognition and creation of value do not happen only in the maker's hands, what kind of work can the work of giving form be called?

When we hear the word design, we picture the work of a maker shaping a form accurately and beautifully. There is a map that set aside that "accurately". The London Underground map that Harry Beck put together in 1931 was not geographically accurate. It did not place the stations or the distances between them as they are above ground; it chose to make it clear at a glance where each line connected and where one could change trains. The Underground company at first rejected it as too radical. When it was issued on trial in 1932 and passengers' opinions were heard, it was well received, and it was printed the following year. What decided the merit of this map was less its accuracy as a map than the way it was used by people looking for connections underground. It can be read that the work of giving form did not end when the map was drawn, but continued until its merit was recognized within the people who used it.

<a id="ldd-design-field"></a><strong>pjdhiro's view (translated by Claude):</strong>Regarding a project's vision, design and way of working, the recognition and creation of value is design work.

The chapters so far have seen that the state of feeling value is in the consciousness of the one who receives, and that creation is the process in which field and fluctuation produce vortices. If so, what the work called design engages with is both what is given form (the vortex) and the field in which it is born and received. Engaging with the field can be read as working with attention not only to the thing given form and the people who make it, but also to the circumstances and relationships around it, and the oppositions and fluctuations in them. Even engaging with the field does not mean vortices are born as one wishes.

<details><summary>More — people who can see only vortices take the fluctuations of the field lightly</summary>

First, from the field of the receiving side. pjdhiro sees it like this.

<strong>pjdhiro's view (translated by Claude):</strong>People who can see only vortices take the fluctuations of the field lightly.

For example, pjdhiro says, a job posting seeking a creator who embeds value in products is not a wrong action. But people who believe in it wholeheartedly, pjdhiro also says, will trouble the creator with nonsensical instructions after hiring. And after that, pjdhiro says, the creator may achieve value creation while being puzzled by the instructions. As a likely scene of nonsensical instructions, pjdhiro gives the following example.

<strong>pjdhiro's example (translated by Claude):</strong>(pjdhiro's words, restated at pjdhiro's request) The maker asks: "We don't need market research, so let me make a good product. I want to see the places where customers actually use it and sense it through empathy, so let me go." To this, the hiring side replies: "There is research that has estimated the field, so look at that report."

pjdhiro sees that [language, symbols and numbers are cut-outs of one aspect, pointing at the vortices of the field](#ldd-vortex). A report is the field put into words and numbers, and is on the side of the vortex. Going to the field is going to touch, with one's body, the fluctuations of the field that produce that vortex.

🚧 How far the difference between the report and the field can be put in the words of vortex and fluctuations of the field is under construction.

</details>

<details><summary>More — on the making side too, not looking only at the vortices producing results</summary>

Next, the field on the making side. The previous chapter read the person who makes as one vortex in the field too. pjdhiro's experience cited in the chapter "[What drives a project?](#ldd-question)" (it seems as if I am trying to make it, but it is born as if the field were swirling into a vortex) was about the results of design and vision. pjdhiro also saw that looking only at the vortex called the individual tends to make one take lightly the field and the oppositions and fluctuations in it. About people and organizations that produce results, pjdhiro says this.

<strong>pjdhiro's view (translated by Claude):</strong>I sometimes see people or organizations that seem to reproduce "producing results in any environment". It might be genius, might be the strength to see things through, might be chance, or they might only be pretending to be able to reproduce it. I often think there could be more viewpoints on "producing results".

🚧 Concrete cases of the field on the making side, and pjdhiro's remarks on them, are under construction.

</details>

<details class="pd-column"><summary>The "design" that the top page of Project Design Theory sets out</summary>

[The top page of Project Design Theory](../index.html), where this page is placed, sets out project design as "the activity of taking as its object, and envisioning and designing, not only the vision, design and execution of a project, but the very methods of execution and management" (translated by Claude). The same page also writes that a project here is an event that includes not only "what we do (Doing)" but also "what is happening (Being)". pjdhiro sees it as envisioning and designing not only what is happening but also what will likely happen and what may happen, and calls this dealing with uncertainty. Then what it engages with is not limited to the content of what is done or the things that get made.

</details>

<details class="pd-column" id="ldd-thinking"><summary><span>Types of thinking, and design rules<span class="pd-column__sub">Logical, process, systems and design thinking, and Baldwin and Clark's modularity</span></span></summary>

Talk of design often brings up ways of thinking that carry the name "thinking", such as design thinking. There are also names like logical thinking, process thinking and systems thinking. Each can be seen as a name given to where one turns one's eyes within the single activity of thinking. Here, what each has been written about as a way of seeing is lined up briefly from representative sources. Which sources were chosen is this page's arrangement.

- **Logical thinking**: spoken of as a way of thinking that traces the connection from grounds to conclusions. In chapter 1 of *How We Think* (1910), the philosopher Dewey called "reflective thought" the active, persistent and careful consideration of a belief in the light of the grounds that support it and the further conclusions to which it tends.
- **Process thinking**: a way of thinking that sees things not as a collection of static individuals but as processes of becoming. The "Process Philosophy" entry of the *Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy* writes that it is a philosophy based on the premise that being is dynamic and that the dynamic nature of being should be the primary focus of any account of reality. The same entry also writes that Western metaphysics has long described reality as a collection of static individuals.
- **Systems thinking**: a way of thinking that looks at the relations between parts, and at the whole, rather than at the parts. The "What is Systems Thinking?" entry of the Systems Engineering Body of Knowledge (SEBoK), citing Senge, writes of it as a discipline for seeing wholes, a framework for seeing interrelationships rather than things. The same entry also writes that there are many examples where drawing the boundaries of scope or time too narrowly solves the present problem but creates the same or a larger problem elsewhere or later.
- **Design thinking**: IDEO's page describes design thinking as a human-centered approach to solving complex problems, in the form of bringing together what is desirable for people, what is viable for the organization, and what is technically feasible. Design thinking is also placed in the table of the separate page "[How far do the ideas of management studies work?](management-drivers.html#デザイン思考)" (in Japanese). An attempt to look at design thinking from the inference Peirce called abduction (inference that goes back from a surprising fact to a hypothesis that explains it) is on the separate page "[Peirce's creative love, and abduction](peirce-love-abduction.html#pa-design)" (in Japanese; 🚧 under construction).

A piece of work that thought about "rules" on the side of design is Baldwin and Clark's *Design Rules, Vol. 1: The Power of Modularity* (2000). Clark was then dean of Harvard Business School. In a conversation the same year, the two named IBM's System/360, launched in the early 1960s, as the first modular family of computers: the parts of the computer could be designed independently by separate specialist teams, and as long as they followed a set of design rules decided in advance, they worked as one when connected.

As long as the rules are followed, what the designers of other parts need not see can be changed freely. The two call this "hidden design parameters". Baldwin also says that such modularity does not arise by chance but has to be designed in. Knowing the rules, anyone can make parts that work in the system. The two name as a major event in the growth of Silicon Valley that after System/360, designers of disk drives left IBM one after another to start their own companies and compete with compatible devices.

Read in this page's words, design rules look closer to arrangements on the side of the field where vortices arise than to the vortices that are each part. In the background of this reader, too, pjdhiro likens a year of work to [refactoring into modules](#ldd-my-app).

Then what moves thinking itself? In the same chapter 1, Dewey placed the origin of thinking in some perplexity, confusion or doubt. He also wrote that thinking is not a case of spontaneous combustion, that there is something that occasions it. pjdhiro sees it this way.

<strong>pjdhiro's view (translated by Claude):</strong>The driving source of thinking is a mystery too. It might be Love. Desire for knowledge, or deficiency, for the sake of a sense of reassurance.

Sources: Dewey (1910) *[How We Think](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/37423)*, Chapter I (full text checked against the public-domain D. C. Heath 1910 edition on Project Gutenberg; sections 1 and 4 of chapter 1) / Seibt (2022) "[Process Philosophy](https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/process-philosophy/)", *Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy* (full text of the public entry checked; the opening section) / Adcock, Wells & Lawson (2026) "[What is Systems Thinking?](https://sebokwiki.org/wiki/What_is_Systems_Thinking%3F)", *SEBoK* (full text checked against the version of the public entry as of 2026-11-13; section Thoughts on Systems Thinking; Senge's wording is this entry's quotation from Senge 2006, pp.68–69) / IDEO "[Design thinking](https://designthinking.ideo.com/)" (full text of the page IDEO publishes checked) / Baldwin & Clark (2000) "[More Than the Sum of Its Parts: The Impact of Modularity on the Computer Industry](https://www.library.hbs.edu/working-knowledge/more-than-the-sum-of-its-parts-the-impact-of-modularity-on-the-computer-industry)", *HBS Working Knowledge*, 2000-09-25 (a conversation introducing their book *Design Rules, Vol. 1*; full text checked against the page HBS publishes; the book itself has not been read)

</details>

<details><summary>Sources</summary>

Beck's map: London Museum "How Harry Beck revolutionised the Tube map" (full text checked against [the page the London Museum publishes](https://www.londonmuseum.org.uk/collections/london-stories/harry-beck-revolutionised-tube-map/); the 1931 map, that it set aside geographical accuracy to make connections and changes easy to understand, that it was at first rejected as too radical, the 1932 trial and passengers' opinions, and that it was printed in 1933).

</details>

## What changes when the input is arranged?

<!--OPEN-->

Then what changes when the input is arranged?

When we hear the word analysis, we picture someone pursuing a single way of seeing to its end. Around us there is also analysis that adds ways of seeing. When a shadow is found on a chest X-ray at a health checkup, the detailed examination uses CT, which goes round the body and layers images taken from many directions, to check the size and depth of the shadow that one picture could not show. A navigation system choosing a route also layers traffic jams, roadworks and the congestion of each time of day over a flat road map, and gives the fastest route. The road that looks shortest on the map can turn out, seen with the layered information, to be the slowest. 3D glasses producing depth from two slightly offset pictures have the same shape. Analysis is not only digging deep into one way of seeing; it is also adding axes of seeing, and seeing what looked flat as a shape with depth.

Arranging the input also has this shape. When one asks of the words and numbers one has received whether one is treating vortices as vortices, then where one had been looking only at shapes with names, one more direction is added: seeing from the side of the field that produces those shapes.

<a id="ldd-effects"></a><strong>pjdhiro's view (translated by Claude):</strong>With many viewpoints, understanding and expressive power become like a number line becoming a plane. …… One is less often misled by things easy to perceive, like vortices.

pjdhiro sees the effects of arranging the input as, at a light estimate, of two kinds: analysis becoming sharper, and biases decreasing. pjdhiro also names others, such as understanding the conditions of application, being able to give consideration to qualitative matters, and becoming stronger against change. As viewpoints and dimensions increase, what was seen as a single row of numbers comes to be seen as a plane, and the error of treating named vortices as things cut off from the field becomes less likely. One can see how far theories and common sense work, and becomes less likely to miss what does not show up in numbers, what happens outside the plan, and what may happen from now on. However, knowing Love-Driven Development does not promise success. Its advantage may lie in not lowering the probability of success. How it works also changes with the cognitive level and personality of the person using it.

<details><summary>More — (1) analysis becomes sharper (looking on two axes, using several ways of seeing)</summary>

<a id="ldd-use-1"></a>As viewpoints and dimensions increase, what was seen as a single row of numbers comes to be seen as a plane. pjdhiro sees understanding and expressive power changing as much as a number line becoming a plane. On this page, [the two signals of survival and trust](#ldd-signals), and [the imaginary axis](#ldd-kesson), one more axis standing at right angles, correspond to those added axes.

Management practice also has tools for looking with more axes. In 1970, Henderson, founder of the Boston Consulting Group, divided products into four by the two axes of market growth rate and market share, and called them cash cows, stars, question marks and pets. The balance of a combination of businesses, invisible with a single yardstick such as growth alone or profit alone, becomes visible.

<details><summary>Note — what became of these two axes</summary>

These two axes do not still apply as they are. In a 2014 retrospective, the same firm wrote that market share no longer predicts performance well, and that the time a business stays in one quadrant shrank, in industries making up 75% of the whole, from about four years in 1992 to less than two years in 2012.

</details>

On the research side, there is research showing that even within one person, giving a second answer by going back to knowledge slightly different from that of the first answer, and averaging the two, reduces error (Herzog and Hertwig, 2009).

<!--REF:Decision-making and bias-->

Sources: Henderson (1970) "[The Product Portfolio](https://www.bcg.com/publications/1970/strategy-the-product-portfolio)", BCG (full text checked against the page BCG publishes) / Reeves, Moose & Venema (2014) "[BCG Classics Revisited: The Growth Share Matrix](https://www.bcg.com/en-gb/publications/2014/growth-share-matrix-bcg-classics-revisited)", BCG (full text checked against the page BCG publishes) / Herzog & Hertwig (2009) "[The wisdom of many in one mind](https://doi.org/10.1111/j.1467-9280.2009.02271.x)", *Psychological Science* 20(2):231 (full text checked against the Wayback Machine record (9 August 2017) of the PDF of the journal pages that was in the library of the Max Planck Institute for Human Development; abstract on p.231).

</details>

<details><summary>More — (2) biases decrease (the error of mistaking the abstract for the concrete, and ways to reduce it)</summary>

<a id="ldd-use-2"></a>One is less often misled by things easy to perceive, like vortices. pjdhiro sees it as a way of dealing with cognitive distortions and limits. On this page, the fold "[Words are names for the vortices seen between the waves](#ldd-vortex)" in the chapter "What shape does creation take?" saw that what people gave names to were shapes easy to perceive. Errors arise easily where a named vortex is treated as a thing cut off from the field.

In chapter 3 of *Science and the Modern World* (1925), the philosopher Whitehead named the error of mistaking the abstract for the concrete "the fallacy of misplaced concreteness". In the same passage Whitehead also wrote that the intellect need not fall into this trap. The direction is not to discard abstractions but to use them knowing they are abstractions. The difference between giving a vortex a name and treating a named vortex as a thing has the same shape as this distinction.

In psychology, ways of reducing bias have been studied. In 2015 Morewedge and colleagues reported that a single training session, with a game or a video, reduced six biases, including confirmation bias and anchoring, by medium to large amounts, and that the effect remained two months later.

The same thing has been said in investment practice. In 2000 Barber and Odean examined the trades of more than 66,000 households and showed that the households that traded most earned an annual return of 11.4%, well below the market's 17.9%, and that this can be explained by overconfidence. In a 1994 talk, the investor Charlie Munger cited the saying "to a man with only a hammer, every problem looks like a nail", and recommended holding the ways of thinking of many fields combined like a latticework. It is an example of speaking of increasing viewpoints and reducing bias as one measure.

<!--REF:Decision-making and bias-->

Sources: Whitehead (1925) *[Science and the Modern World](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/68611)*, Chapter III (full text checked against the public-domain Macmillan 1925 edition on Project Gutenberg; chapter III, p.72 by the book's index) / Morewedge, Yoon, Scopelliti, Symborski, Korris & Kassam (2015) "[Debiasing decisions](https://openaccess.city.ac.uk/id/eprint/12324/)", *Policy Insights from the Behavioral and Brain Sciences* 2(1):129 (full text checked against the authors' manuscript in the institutional repository of City, University of London; manuscript pp.2–3) / Barber & Odean (2000) "[Trading is hazardous to your wealth](https://faculty.haas.berkeley.edu/odean/Papers%20current%20versions/Individual_Investor_Performance_Final.pdf)", *Journal of Finance* 55(2):773 (full text checked against the Wayback Machine record (6 October 2026) of the PDF of the journal pages on author Odean's University of California, Berkeley page; abstract on p.773) / Munger (1994) "[A Lesson on Elementary, Worldly Wisdom](https://www.stripe.press/poor-charlies-almanack/book)", talk at the University of Southern California (full text checked against Talk Two of the web edition of *Poor Charlie's Almanack* published by Stripe Press).

</details>

<details><summary>More — others: understanding the conditions of application</summary>

<a id="ldd-use-3"></a>One comes to see how far theories and common sense work. pjdhiro sees this as a major contribution of this reader.

<strong>pjdhiro's view (translated by Claude):</strong>By lining up the major existing cultures, values and viewpoints, putting into words what each is driven by, and coming to know what I want to call Love in order to deal with things they cannot explain or that don't go well with them, won't we notice that what was held to be good was conditional?

As examples where those conditions come into view, pjdhiro names metacognition and taking others' perspectives.

<strong>pjdhiro's view (translated by Claude):</strong>For example, metacognition is the objectification of the self, and taking others' perspectives means growth for a certain stage of development, but for people who have already reached that stage it means stagnation. In this way we can pin down the range in which existing theories work.

Whether taking others' perspectives is good changes, in pjdhiro's view, with which stage of development the person is at. [The question of profit and the long term](#ldd-profit) is also one pjdhiro gave as an example of something held to be good being a conditional right answer. In this page's tables, each condition is written in the "Situations where it works" column of [the management studies table](#ldd-management) and the "Conditions under which it works" row of [the tables of the words of the mind](#ldd-words).

<details><summary>More — objectifying the self, others' perspectives, and what can be taken as an object at each stage</summary>

In consciousness research, metacognition is written of as a higher-order process that takes cognition as its object (Overgaard and Sandberg, 2012): the work of looking at what one is thinking as an object of thought.

Research on adult development writes that what at one stage one sees from within and cannot take as an object comes to be seen as an object at the next stage. O'Fallon defines a stage as a perspective, and counts it by which person-perspective one can take (2020, p.18; the chapter "[What looks threatening changes with the stage of development](#ldd-stages)"). One's own thoughts and values, too, are invisible while one sees from within them, and become visible when they become objects. One's self, too, gradually comes to be seen as an object in this transition.

What comes to be seen as an object at each stage is laid out, as a summary of O'Fallon's definitions, in the table "What can and cannot be taken as an object" in [What are stages of mental development?](developmental-stages.html#何を対象にできるかできないか) (in Japanese). Picking out what concerns others' perspectives and how oneself appears: at 2.0, how oneself appears from the other; at 2.5, the other's feelings; at 3.0, oneself seen from a little distance; at 4.0, oneself thinking, and that one's values were formed by upbringing and culture; at 4.5, how the other sees oneself, and one's own meaning-making mechanism, come to be seen as objects.

Cook-Greuter's descriptions of the stages also show the handling of others' views stage by stage. At the Expert stage, losing one's current certainty by opening to others' views becomes a fear (2013, pp.39–40). At the Pluralist stage, many perspectives are visible, and it is written that when fairness and respect for voices are too strong, one becomes unable to decide for fear of being seen as one-sided (pp.53, 58–59). For people at the earlier stage, taking others' perspectives amounts to going beyond fear and seeing what could not yet be taken as an object; for people who already see many perspectives, it can also amount to staying where they are.

🚧 Fitting pjdhiro's growth and stagnation to these two stage descriptions like this is Claude's reading and has not yet been confirmed with pjdhiro. Checking the idea of subject and object against the full text of Kegan's original (1982) is also under construction.

<!--REF:Psychology-->

Sources: [Overgaard & Sandberg (2012)](https://doi.org/10.1098/rstb.2011.0425) "Kinds of access: different methods for report reveal different kinds of metacognitive access", *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B* 367(1594):1287 (full text checked against the Wayback Machine record (27 October 2019) of the publisher's PDF; p.1287; the record of the checking is in the literature record (consciousness and awareness) of "[What Is Consciousness](https://uminomae.github.io/project-design-theory/as/)", in Japanese) / O'Fallon (2020) "[States and STAGES: Waking up Developmentally](https://integral-review.org/issues/vol_16_no_1_ofallon_states_and_stages.pdf)", *Integral Review* 16(1) (full text checked; defining stages as perspectives p.18) / Cook-Greuter (2013) "[Nine Levels of Increasing Embrace in Ego Development](https://web.archive.org/web/20211028144039/http://www.cook-greuter.com/Cook-Greuter%209%20levels%20paper%20new%201.1'14%2097p%5B1%5D.pdf)", prepublication version (full text checked; Expert pp.39–40, Pluralist pp.53, 58–59).

</details>

</details>

<details><summary>More — others: being able to give consideration to qualitative matters (when only what is easy to measure is measured)</summary>

<a id="ldd-use-4"></a>One becomes less likely to miss what does not show up in numbers. This page saw that values on [the imaginary axis](#ldd-kesson) are read as 0 on the symbols. Value bearing on trust, and value made of as many axes as there are adjectives, are also easily dropped when put into numbers.

In a 1976 paper, the social psychologist Campbell wrote that the more any quantitative social indicator is used for social decision-making, the more subject it will be to corruption pressures and the more apt it will be to distort and corrupt the social processes it is intended to monitor. The moment things are put into numbers, what does not fit into numbers goes outside the watch.

<!--REF:Decision-making and bias-->

Sources: Campbell (1976) "[Assessing the Impact of Planned Social Change](https://jmde.journals.publicknowledgeproject.org/index.php/jmde_1/article/view/297)", Dartmouth College Occasional Paper 8 (also in *Evaluation and Program Planning* 2(1):67, 1979; full text checked against the linked reprint in *Journal of MultiDisciplinary Evaluation* 7(15), 2011 (the open-access journal publisher's version); p.34).

</details>

<details><summary>More — others: becoming stronger against change (dots connect looking backward)</summary>

<a id="ldd-use-5"></a>One becomes able to receive what happens outside the plan without missing it, and can be ready for changes that may happen, before they happen. On this page, [dots connecting within one person and the field fluctuating](#ldd-question), pjdhiro's view that [the kickoff comes after expectation is betrayed](#ldd-expect), and pjdhiro's view of resilience as a process in which a new self is created ([the tables of the words of the mind](#ldd-words)) connect here.

On 12 June 2005, in a commencement speech at Stanford University, Steve Jobs told how a calligraphy class Jobs dropped in on after leaving college connected ten years later to the beautiful typography of the first Macintosh, and said that you cannot connect the dots looking forward, you can only connect them looking backward, so you have to trust that the dots will somehow connect in your future. pjdhiro sees feeling as if preparation within one person had already begun as close to this story.

<!--REF:Decision-making and bias-->

Sources: Jobs (2005) "[You've got to find what you love](https://news.stanford.edu/stories/2005/06/youve-got-find-love-jobs-says)", Stanford University commencement speech (full text in the Stanford Report, 14 June 2005; full text checked against the linked page and Wayback Machine records of the original page (15 May 2024, 17 June 2005)).

</details>

<details><summary>More — the range in which it works, and fit</summary>

pjdhiro likens Love-Driven Development to test code.

<strong>pjdhiro's view (translated by Claude):</strong>It's one of many pieces of test code. There are things my theory can break down. Like clearing up misunderstandings. It differs with the user's cognitive level and personality too. There is such a thing as fit.

As the quotation says, how it works changes with the cognitive level and personality of the person using it. As written about the people who make, in the value creation section of the chapter "[Where are the words of business misunderstood?](#ldd-creation)", the person using it can also be seen as one vortex. Those who use it are not necessarily managers or those who give instructions.

</details>

<details><summary>More — for those who receive instructions</summary>

pjdhiro says there are also clients who push "should" arguments, such as "a professional should be able to make it without doing such research". This can be read as the view that creation is something managed by people's ability, coming out as a "should" argument.

<strong>pjdhiro's view (translated by Claude):</strong>Maybe it's a way of surviving so as not to get dragged in by clients like that.

If one understands that the instruction comes from seeing value only on the side of symbols and creation only on the side of ability, one may be spared from taking the "should" argument as one's own inadequacy.

</details>

<details><summary>More — making money, and being able to recognize value</summary>

<a id="ldd-profit-aware"></a>
pjdhiro sees value as born in the customer's consciousness and changing with the customer's knowledge and consciousness. pjdhiro sees that if society does not recognize pollution or side effects, even bad products have value; selling products full of whitewashing and side effects makes more money, and those who know about it don't make money because of their ethics.

<strong>pjdhiro's view (translated by Claude):</strong>Making money in business and being able to recognize value are separate. Often you make more money not knowing. It is also a realization that it's decided by fit with customers.

Without judging by good and bad, one can look, as a structure, at what is recognized as value.

</details>

## The answer of the second half

<!--OPEN-->

<a id="ldd-development-answer"></a>At the head of the second half, we asked again what the word development points to. Here, for now, is what can be said from the chapters so far in answer to the question in this part's title.

Following the answer of the first half, what moves the whole of what is called development may be the gap, the lack, between what I want to call Love and oneself (the chapter "[That is the driving source of projects](#ldd-conclusion)"). What people can do within it is to check the words and numbers they have received before applying theories or instructions (arranging the input). How to use this is in the chapter "[How are the words of development checked with this view?](#ldd-screen)", and its effects are in the chapter "[What changes when the input is arranged?](#ldd-effects)".

<a id="ldd-debug"></a>The work of development has stages of checking and fixing what was made, such as testing, debugging and refactoring. These are often spoken of as unglamorous work for maintaining quality, and tend to be taken as something separate from creation, which produces something new. What are the stages of checking and fixing for?

<strong>pjdhiro's view (translated by Claude):</strong>Debugging and refactoring may also exist for creation. At least in the sense of self-renewal. I think it gains something like mental strength that doesn't let others manipulate your life and doesn't create hesitation in your thinking. It doesn't mean not listening to others. It's for taking them as reference and including them.

In this view, the stages of checking and fixing are directed not only at what was made but also at the person making it. Rechecking one's own knowledge and experience, and continuing to be oneself while rearranging what is inside, is self-renewal. It could also be called the creation of the self. What gets fixed is the gaps in the mind through which manipulative others get in, something like an individual's security holes. The freedom from hesitation that grows there is not for shutting out outside voices. It can be read that because one can stay intact while receiving outside voices, one can take them as reference and take them in. pjdhiro sees this not breaking down as the power to provide psychological safety to the field. Psychological safety is not an ability inside one person but something shared in the field ([What is psychological safety?](psychological-safety-en.html)). It can be read that an individual's power connects to the psychological safety of the field in this direction: when there is someone who does not break down on receiving outside voices, the other people in that field also find it easier to speak up.

Then what kind of state is staying intact while receiving outside voices?

<strong>pjdhiro's view (translated by Claude):</strong>Keeping the general-purpose module called me open so that it accepts any input, and because of that my behavior gets busy with error handling — something like conditional branches grows for processing that fast, without bugs.

If you narrow the input, what does not fit is rejected at the entrance, and processing becomes easier. In exchange, what can be received becomes narrow. If you keep the input open, what does not fit also comes in, so the work of handling it after receiving increases. In this view, that way of handling grows within the stages of checking and fixing. Errors can be read less as something to avoid than as material from which the way of handling grows. The patterns of the mind's processing seen in the chapter "[The mind has mechanisms to protect itself](#ldd-defense)" can also be read as being rearranged in this way.

<details><summary>More — is creation born in unglamorous stages?</summary>

pjdhiro says that even amid a climate demanding new, ambitious and creative things, pjdhiro has kept debugging their own knowledge and experience, as usual.

<strong>pjdhiro's view (translated by Claude):</strong>Doing something creative may not only be having AI make something in a creative atmosphere. It may not be sparkly. It may be born in the unglamorous stages of testing, quality control and debugging. Like gears spinning round and round with inverse elements.

<strong>pjdhiro's view (translated by Claude):</strong>To do ambitious, creative things, I keep debugging. Myself. Like refactoring, like code aiming for consistent behavior of "the app called 'me'". Test-driven development too. You could also say it raises security. So nothing intrudes. So it doesn't get hacked. Reality, that is.

The view likening oneself to an "app" also appears in words looking back on this year's work (see also: "the app called me" in [How I came to this question — background and history](#ldd-background)).

The protection in the second remark points in a different direction from protection that closes the entrance. It is protection in which one's behavior does not break down because one keeps running the stages of checking and fixing. In the entry "[Value creation](#ldd-creation)", we saw pjdhiro's view that fluctuations may be opposing within one person, and conflict may be producing something. The stages of checking and fixing can also be read as work that repeatedly runs the receiving and handling of that opposition.

</details>

<details><summary>More — a book that wrote of the creation of the self as the most important creation</summary>

<a id="ldd-self-creation"></a>The way of speaking that calls renewing the self creation is also found in a book describing personality types. In their 1996 book on the Enneagram, Riso and Hudson, where they describe the healthy form of the type that lives toward art and beauty (Type Four), write that creation is not only for artists but something everyone should bring about within themselves. On that basis, they put forward that the most important creation is renewing oneself by going beyond the ego, turning everything, good and bad, into something for growing as a person (pp.144–145).

The same book writes, as the form of the type when it moves in a healthy direction, that what is made becomes objective, and the maker can learn from it what is true about themselves. What is made may be a work of art, an act of kindness, or a relationship that went well (p.167).

"because integrating Fours have transcended themselves, what they create is objective, something from which they can learn what is true about themselves." (p.167)

This seems to point in the same direction as this chapter's reading that the stages of checking and fixing are directed not only at what was made but also at the person making it. Riso and Hudson write it as a description of a type, not about the process of development work. Setting the two side by side like this is Claude's analogy.

</details>

<details><summary>Sources</summary>

## What is still not known

<!--OPEN-->

In its second half, the sister reader "Isn't Love like gravity?" asked what the role of a manager is ([chapter 5](gravity-love.html#gl-hypothesis), in Japanese). From there, questions not yet answered remain.

<a id="ldd-open"></a><strong>pjdhiro's question (translated by Claude):</strong>The role of a manager is decision-making. But the role concerning being is not yet clarified. Could it be that being itself is the source?

<a id="ldd-conclusion-being"></a>This reader has seen the driving source as the distortion of the field people are in, but where in this picture the people who decide and move things, managers for example, are is not yet known. The view that the very presence of the person who decides makes the shape of the well's hollow, and the view that that person too is one point drawn to the center and orbiting it, can hold at the same time. Just as a planet orbits the sun while having moons orbit around it, people can be drawn as being moved within the distortion of the field while also having a small hollow around themselves. The first view, of whether being itself is the source, is left as a question, neither adopted nor dropped. Besides the hypothesis that there may be no good and bad or rationality (the fold below), the questions left open are as follows.

- Do between-ness among people, between places and between events appear from the same division, or is another line needed?
- Is the signal of trust itself [the stability of the umbilical cord](#ldd-love-umbilical)?
- Besides the four, are there explorations that touch other parts of the elephant?

<details><summary>More — is being itself the source?</summary>

The sister reader "Isn't Love like gravity?" sets two views side by side here ([chapter 4](gravity-love.html#gl-manager), [chapter 5](gravity-love.html#gl-hypothesis), in Japanese). One is the view that the very presence of the person who decides makes the shape of the well's hollow. In the word decision-making, deciding and moving things, and the presence of the person who decides, are still contained undivided. It reads that dividing off the side of movement reveals a layer that does not move but decides the shape around it. The other is the view that that person too is one point drawn to the center and orbiting it.

<strong>pjdhiro's view (translated by Claude):</strong>Even managers may be drawn by a central driving source like love.

The two can hold at the same time (the picture of the planet and its moons above). "People may be part of a field I want to call Love, and inside its distortion" in "[That is the driving source of projects](#ldd-conclusion)" stands on the second view.

</details>

<details><summary>More — looking at the place before division without dividing it</summary>

In working on a mechanism for processing emotions, the yardsticks of good and bad gradually came off ("From emotion processing to what is not two" in [Background and history](#ldd-background)). From there, pjdhiro holds the following question.

<strong>pjdhiro's question (translated by Claude):</strong>Might there be no good and bad, no rationality?

People gather because it is a good vision. Things proceed because it is a rational plan. Beyond the vision, such explanations work well. Good and bad and rationality are judgments made on the axis of thinking and deciding. But if the driving source lies at right angles to that axis, and is read as 0 on the axis of thinking and deciding, then the yardsticks of good or bad, rational or not, may not apply in the first place to the attraction that wells up there.

The moment one divides into good and evil, rational and irrational, that itself becomes one dualism. What can be seen when one tries to look at the place before division without dividing it? This question is still a question.
</details>

<details><summary>More — the question of the "between" in human, space and time</summary>

At the root of the view of dividing oneself, pjdhiro places the following question ([chapter 6](gravity-love.html#gl-roots) of the sister reader "Isn't Love like gravity?", in Japanese).

<strong>pjdhiro's question (translated by Claude):</strong>What if a human (*ningen*) exists in the between of person and person, and the self (*jibun*) exists where one has divided oneself? I think a question like that is at the root. The word *jibun* expresses exactly that.

(In Japanese, *ningen* (human) is written with the characters for "person" and "between", and *jibun* (self) with the characters for "oneself" and "part, divide".)

This question is within the picture of the gravity well. As seen in the fold "[Placing the explorations so far on the single picture of the gravity well](#ldd-summary)" in the chapter "What is what I want to call Love like?", pjdhiro sees oneself, standing in the hollow, as being among people. The four explorations lined up in this reader can be read as lying on the line of dividing oneself within one person. Whether several centers orbiting one another's wells can be written with the same line is not yet known.

The three Japanese words for human (*ningen*), space (*kūkan*) and time (*jikan*) all contain the character 間, "between". Asked what pjdhiro feels in the between of person and person, pjdhiro gave the following experience.

<a id="ldd-between"></a><strong>pjdhiro's experience (translated by Claude):</strong>Other people's feelings and viewpoints sometimes appear inside me, as if contagious, as if copied. Before I think it, it has already happened. For example, it isn't like looking at someone and thinking "they look sad"; without noticing, it has become "I am sad", and I notice afterward. It may be an illusion, but that's how I feel it. Atmosphere, or sensing, or empathy — as if there were something in the between of person and person.

Psychology and neuroscience have called something close to this way of appearing emotional contagion. De Vignemont and Singer (2006) defined empathy narrowly: a state in which one's own emotion is isomorphic to the other's emotion, and one knows that the other is its source. On that basis, they separate emotional contagion as a state in which emotion is shared but self and other are not distinguished. Their example is an infant who starts crying when other infants cry. pjdhiro's experience can be read as a flow in which an emotion that appeared without distinction is distinguished afterward. The record of checking this theory is in the intersubjectivity record of "[What Is Consciousness](https://uminomae.github.io/project-design-theory/as/)" (in Japanese). How close words such as empathy and emotional contagion are used differently is in the table of the separate page "[What is empathy?](empathy.html#em-usage)" (in Japanese).

<!--REF:Nerves and emotion-->

Sources: de Vignemont & Singer (2006) "[The empathic brain: how, when and why?](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.tics.2006.08.008)", *Trends in Cognitive Sciences* 10(10):435–441 (full text checked against the authors' manuscript in the institutional repository HAL; the four conditions of empathy, and that emotional contagion does not meet the condition of distinguishing self and other p.435).

About space and time too, pjdhiro has experiences of what had been divided wavering.

<strong>pjdhiro's experience (translated by Claude):</strong>With time and space too, reading the theory of relativity breaks down my common sense. What I naively thought was space turns out to be something like time, and what I thought was time turns out to be something like space.

In special relativity, how much of the separation between two events counts as time and how much as space changes with the motion of the observer. Two events that happen at the same time in separate places for one observer happen at different times for an observer moving relative to them. So this theory writes space and time combined into one, as spacetime.

Claude reads the two experiences as follows. Both happen where the division between things one had seen as divided is not as firm as one thought. People are separate bodies, but emotions sometimes appear between them before a distinction is made. Space and time, too, are counted separately in everyday life, but how they are divided changes with the motion of the observer. This page has looked at what I want to call Love as a field before division. Human, space and time may be three "betweens" that become visible when people divide that field.

</details>

<!--TURN-->

Part 6 looked at what is happening inside what is called development. That is the second half of the reader.

Part 7 brings the first and second halves together and looks at what Love-Driven Development, as I want to call it, is. First, through a summary and an arrangement, it sets out a definition. Next, it adds to this from the viewpoints of education, computer science, philosophy and Zen. Finally, supposing the reasoning is understood, it looks at what to do now, as practice: the situations in which to use this view, how to train the skill, and how to practice. Practice is also where the second question of the opening, "Why do we try to become something different from what we were?", is approached from the side of developing oneself.

<!--/TURN-->

<!--PART:Part 7|What I want to call Love-Driven Development-->

## What I want to call Love-Driven Development

<!--OPEN-->

<a id="ldd-definition"></a>Then what is what I want to call Love-Driven Development?

Of the words in the title, this reader looked at Love and driving in the first half, and at development in the second half. This chapter bundles them together and places, by summarizing and arranging, what it is that I want to call Love-Driven Development. The answer of the first half was placed, for now, in the chapter "[That is the driving source of projects](#ldd-conclusion)", and the answer of the second half in the chapter "[The answer of the second half](#ldd-development-answer)".

About what this view has been looking for, pjdhiro puts it as follows.

<strong>pjdhiro's view (translated by Claude):</strong>Maybe I was looking for two things: the "method" of decision-making and judgment, and a source like truth (or the driving source of that existence).

<a id="ldd-tree-rings"></a>Then from where does this view see the whole? About what happens when one tries to express this view and the five stages of creation as a single viewpoint, pjdhiro sees it as follows.

<strong>pjdhiro's view (translated by Claude):</strong>If I express Love-Driven Development or the five stages of creation as a viewpoint, it seems it would turn into something paradoxical: seeing the whole by seeing the here and now.

This paradox can be seen in one picture by comparing it to the growth rings of a tree. The cross-section of a stump is one surface cut through the tree as it is now. On that surface, all the years from the year it sprouted to this year are lined up as rings. The thin rings of years when it did not grow much and the thick rings of years when it grew well are on the same surface. Looking down on the forest from the sky is also one way of seeing the whole. Growth rings are a different way: seeing the whole from the past to now by looking at the present cross-section.

The view of this reader also has a shape close to growth rings. A vortex visible here and now (one event, or one feeling) reflects the fluctuations of the field beneath it and the interactions up to then. Still, what growth rings reflect is the time of one tree, not the whole forest. The viewpoints of education, computer science, philosophy and Zen in the following chapters also each look at this view as one cross-section.

🚧 Under construction: a definition that bundles the two answers together will be written.

## Seen from education

<a id="ldd-education"></a>Seen from the viewpoint of education, how does the view of this reader look?

When one thinks of a school class, the picture that comes to mind is the teacher explaining at the front and the students listening at their desks. Knowledge flows from the teacher who knows to the students who do not yet know. In the field of education, separately from this, a form in which learners teach one another and learn from one another has been studied under the name peer learning. Peer means a companion of equal standing. In a 1999 paper, the higher-education researchers Sampson, Boud and colleagues call students working together to learn with and from each other reciprocal peer learning. They write that the difference from the form in which one of the peers teaches in place of the teacher (peer tutoring) is that the power and status of the students is similar and the emphasis is on learning together.

About this form of learning from one another, pjdhiro sees it as follows.

<strong>pjdhiro's view (translated by Claude):</strong>You could also call it the Love-Driven Development of the education field. In programming terms, it's pair programming.

In the field of education, many methods that build learning from one another into classes have been tried. Four are lined up here. Pair programming in programming is looked at in the next chapter, "[Seen from computer science](#ldd-pair-programming)".

- Peer Instruction: a method the physicist Mazur and colleagues have kept using at Harvard University in physics lectures for students not majoring in physics. The teacher poses a multiple-choice question that asks about concepts. Students give their own answer in one or two minutes, and then, with the people around them, explain their reasoning and try to convince one another. Finally, they answer again. The number of people answering correctly after the discussion rose greatly. But that was when the share of correct answers before the discussion was between 35% and 70%, that is, when people who answered correctly and people who answered wrongly were mixed. Crouch and Mazur write that when the share of correct answers is lower than that, too few people understand enough for a fruitful discussion, and when it is higher, there is little to gain from discussing.
- Reciprocal Teaching: a method for growing the ability to comprehend texts, reported by the psychologists Palincsar and Brown in 1984. It was aimed at seventh graders (equivalent to the first year of junior high school in Japan) who were poor at comprehension. Four activities, summarizing, asking questions, clarifying what is hard to understand, and predicting what comes next, are carried out with the teacher and students taking turns in the role of "teacher". At first the adult teacher modeled them, and even when their turn came, the students could not handle the role of leading well. By the end of ten sessions, the students were giving paraphrases and questions of some sophistication. The core of this method is the alternation between adult and students. Students modeling for one another appeared in the second study, carried out with larger groups.
- Peer Response: in a 1999 paper, Reiko Ikeda, a researcher in Japanese-language education, studied an activity in which learners read one another's compositions and discussed them while revising. In the discussion between learners, there were utterances that moved the discussion forward and utterances that softened the way things were said. In one-on-one guidance with the teacher, such utterances were not seen. Ikeda reads this as the learners themselves controlling the learning activity.
- Cooperative Learning: the researchers Johnson and Johnson, in a 2009 paper, summarize research on cooperation, competition and working individually. They write that when people feel their goals are linked, that is, that they can reach their own goal only when the people they work with reach theirs, interactions of encouraging and helping one another arise. This is called positive interdependence.

What the four have in common is that they do not leave the role of teaching with the teacher alone, but pass it around among the learners or have people of similar ability talk with each other. Read in the words of this reader, these methods work on the shape of the interactions in the field called the classroom before they work on the content taught. The ways they work on it are linking goals, passing roles around, and evening out standing. Read that way, learning comes to look like something that wells up within those interactions. That, in Ikeda's study, the very way the discussion proceeded changed when learners were of the same standing can be read as one example of this. It points in the same direction as the next chapter's view that the system that gets built reflects the interactions of the people who build it.

<details><summary>In more detail: the procedures of the four methods, and how far they were confirmed</summary>

In Peer Instruction, the discussion usually lasts two to four minutes. Answers to the questions are not graded; a few points are given for continued participation. On a test of concepts in mechanics, the average of the value that measures gain (normalized gain) rose from 0.25 in 1990, with conventional lectures, to 0.49 in 1991, when this method was introduced. Most students who changed their answer in the discussion changed from a wrong answer to a right one; 6% changed from a right answer to a wrong one. No student answered more than 80% of the questions correctly before the discussion.

The form of the Reciprocal Teaching procedure is grounded in the developmental psychologist Vygotsky's zone of proximal development. The zone of proximal development is the distance between the level a child can reach alone and the level at which the child can take part when guided by someone who knows more. Palincsar and Brown write that the adult eventually hands over the main part of the thinking role to the child and takes the position of a supportive, sympathetic listener. In the dialogue on day 3 of the second study, the students interacted only with the teacher, but on day 13, encouraged by the adult teacher, the students were responding to one another.

Ikeda's study analyzes the case of one pair of learners. She also writes, as a limit, that for exchanges about the content of the compositions, the intermediate learners' knowledge of the language was not enough.

Johnson and Johnson list five things that determine how well cooperation works: positive interdependence, individual accountability, promotive interaction, the appropriate use of social skills, and group processing. They also write that there have been more than 1,200 studies of cooperation, competition and individual work over 110 years.

Sampson, Boud and colleagues distinguish cooperative learning, in which the teacher holds the authority of knowledge, from collaborative learning, which puts weight on the learners' participation and on the teaching side sharing control, and they see reciprocal peer learning as part of collaborative learning. They write that the teacher's role becomes more indirect, moving to the side of facilitating, but is still essential.

</details>

## Seen from computer science

<!--OPEN-->

<a id="ldd-computing"></a>Seen from the viewpoint of computer science, how does the view of this reader look?

The workings of a computer seem to be decided by the logic of the machine and to have nothing to do with the circumstances of the people and organizations that build it. The programmer Conway, in a paper published in 1968 in the magazine *Datamation*, gives an example like this. In a contract research organization, eight people were to build translating programs (compilers) for two programming languages, COBOL and ALGOL. After estimating the work, they assigned five people to COBOL and three to ALGOL. The COBOL compiler that resulted ran in five phases, and the ALGOL compiler ran in three. Conway writes that organizations which design systems are constrained to produce designs which are copies of the communication structures of these organizations. This view later came to be called Conway's law.

<a id="ldd-inverse-conway"></a>This law is also used in reverse. If there is a shape of system one wants, one changes the way teams are divided first; this is called the inverse Conway maneuver. LeRoy and Simons named it in 2010.

Put into the words of Love-Driven Development, what gets built (the vortex) reflects the interactions of the people who build it (field to wave). So if one wants to change what gets built, one works first on the interactions. The inverse Conway maneuver is one way of working on them.

Still, interactions are not decided by the lines of an organization chart alone. Things in the same direction have been said under other names too.

- DevOps: in 2009, Allspaw and Hammond of the photo-sharing site Flickr told of how development and operations, by joining forces, became able to release new versions more than ten times a day. What they named as decisive, alongside tools, was culture, in four points: respect for each other's expertise, trust in each other, a healthy attitude that failures will happen, and avoiding blame.
- Amoeba management: Kazuo Inamori of Kyocera divided the organization into small groups run with independent accounting, created buying and selling between the groups, and made a form in which each person takes part in management. Inamori writes that its foundation is the human heart.

Both, while changing the form (the way teams are divided, the units of accounting), work on the trust and the heart beneath it. Love-Driven Development stands on the side of looking at the layer beneath that, that is, at what is welling up in the field.

Then, like the inverse Conway maneuver, if one first built an organization in the shape of Love-Driven Development, what shape would the information system that results take? Let us think about it as a thought experiment (Claude's reading).

First, the organization in the shape of Love-Driven Development is described in three points in the words of this reader. First, rather than fitting into the boxes of roles, people gather when needed and come apart when their task is done, like vortices swirling in the [field](#ldd-field). Second, what moves people is [signals of trust](#ldd-signals) rather than instructions or evaluations. Third, people of different standing can voice their differences and bring them up against each other. The edge of the five stages of creation, the border where heterogeneous things meet and collide, is out in the open, not hidden.

Following Conway's view, the information system that results reflects this shape.

- It becomes one shared field: it would be unlikely to take the form of business systems closed off by department and connected by stages of approval and transcription. It might take a form like one field, in which everyone sees the same data and the same work in progress, and anyone can work on it.
- Parts are born and come apart: just as vortices swirl and come apart, the parts of the system might also take a form premised on being made small and removed when no longer needed. Conway himself concludes that because the design which occurs first is almost never the best possible, flexibility of organization is important.
- The seams can be decided again: Conway writes that if two parts of a system are connected, the two groups that built them must have negotiated and agreed upon the way of connecting (the interface). In an organization where differences can be voiced, the seams between parts might become something that can be discussed and decided again any number of times.
- Records and notices become things for learning: rather than records kept to leave who was to blame, or notices for reporting upward, the center might be records for everyone to look at what happened and learn, and notices that show anomalies to everyone quickly. Allspaw, who was an engineer at the online marketplace Etsy, wrote in 2012 that an engineer who thinks they are going to be reprimanded stops giving the details needed to understand what happened.

The reverse direction can be thought about with the same view. In an organization where safety is not felt and signals of survival well up strongly, the walls between departments, the layering of approval stages, and the records kept to leave responsibility might become the shape of the system as they are. Conway writes that an organization whose communication is not flexible will stamp out an image of itself in every design it produces.

<details><summary>In more detail: has the view that the shapes of organization and system resemble each other been confirmed?</summary>

Conway's view was later studied as the mirroring hypothesis. MacCormack and colleagues compared pairs of software with the same function and size, one made by a loosely coupled organization (open source) and one by a tightly coupled organization (a company or a small dedicated team). In every pair across five fields, the one made by the loosely coupled organization was more divided into parts. In the degree to which a change in one part spreads to others, there was a difference of up to a factor of eight. The pairs compared were in five fields only.

In 2016, Colfer and Baldwin brought together 142 studies. Among studies describing companies and industries, 70% strongly supported the hypothesis, 22% partly supported it, and 8% did not support it. On the other hand, among studies describing open collaborative projects such as open source, 56% did not support it. There, tightly intertwined code was being built by people with thin organizational ties. What worked in its place was that the work in progress was visible to anyone and anyone could work on it. This is close to the form like one shared field in the thought experiment above. It also means that the shape of the organization is not the only thing that decides the shape of the system.

</details>

<a id="ldd-conway-ai"></a>Then can such an information system be built? About the four forms of the thought experiment, pjdhiro guesses as follows.

<strong>pjdhiro's guess (translated by Claude):</strong>My guess is that it becomes possible with AI coming in. Don't you think?

Many of the four forms have until now been hard to build because the work was heavy. AI is lowering, among that work, the work of reading and understanding and the work of writing and rewriting.

- One shared field: even if everyone sees the same thing, if the words and assumptions differ by department, it cannot be read. Even in the open collaborative projects in the fold above, those who could understand the work in progress and work on it were limited to people who could read code. In a study that brought AI assistance into a customer support desk, those who improved most were the less experienced people. The authors read this as AI perhaps spreading the hard-to-put-into-words knowledge of skilled people to new people. If AI rephrases and summarizes, the range of people who can understand the work in progress might widen to beyond the specialty.
- Parts that are born and come apart, and seams that can be decided again: making parts and throwing them away, and changing the way of connecting and rewriting both sides, take the work of writing code. There is an experiment in which programmers using AI assistance finished the same task much faster. If the work of rewriting goes down, there is less reason to keep things in a form that cannot be moved once decided.
- Records for learning, and notices visible to everyone: for everyone to learn what happened takes the work of reading and summarizing many records. Here too, AI seems able to lower the work of reading.

Still, AI does not change what is welling up in the field. If an organization where safety is not felt brings in AI, AI might work not as a field visible to everyone but as a tool for watching over people. Following Conway's view, a system that uses AI also reflects the communication of the organization that builds it. There is also work AI is not good at. There is an experiment in which, on tasks outside the range AI is good at, the people using AI produced correct answers at a lower rate.

When AI comes to work inside organizations, the communication reflected in the system also includes the exchanges between people and AI. This reader too is written through an exchange in which Claude researches and answers pjdhiro's questions (the back-matter chapter "[How the collaboration with AI proceeds](#ldd-ai)").

<details><summary>In more detail: the numbers on the effect of AI assistance, and their limits</summary>

Brynjolfsson and colleagues brought generative AI assistance to 5,179 people at a customer support desk and compared. The number of issues resolved per hour rose by 14% on average. The bottom fifth in skill rose by 35%, and the most skilled people did not rise. These numbers are from the 2023 version; in the version revised in 2024, the number of people and the figures change slightly.

Peng and colleagues gave 95 programmers the task of writing an HTTP server in JavaScript, and divided them into a group using AI assistance (GitHub Copilot) and a group not using it. The group using it finished the task 55.8% faster. They write that the effect was larger for people with less programming experience.

Dell'Acqua and colleagues tested this with 758 consultants. On tasks within the range AI is good at, the people using AI completed 12.2% more tasks on average, 25.1% faster, with quality more than 40% higher. On tasks outside that range, the people using AI were 19 percentage points less likely to produce correct answers. The authors call the border of the range AI is good at the jagged frontier, and write that even tasks that seem equally difficult fall on either side, inside and outside the range.

</details>

<a id="ldd-pair-programming"></a>The smallest form of interaction is two people. In software development there is a practice called pair programming. Two people sit at one computer and write one program. One holds the keyboard and writes, and the other watches that work from the side, looking for errors, thinking of other options, and thinking about the way ahead. The two switch roles from time to time. Williams and colleagues tried this practice in a university course and reported in 2000. Once used to it, the pairs spent at minimum only 15% more person-hours than writing alone, and the share passing the automated tests was higher than for individuals in every one of four assignments. But the students who tried it in pairs were all people who had wanted to pair of their own accord.

Teaching one another also happens inside pair programming. Cockburn and Williams write that the two take turns being the teacher and the taught, from moment to moment. In 2015, Plonka and colleagues studied the work of people who pair-program at their jobs through video and audio recordings. The experienced people used six ways of teaching, from subtle hints to explicit instructions. The experienced side, too, reconsidered their own assumptions from the novices' naive questions. Zuill, who started mob programming, in which the whole team sits at one computer, writes that the team holds as a principle always treating each other with kindness, consideration and respect, and thinks of learning as one kind of contribution to the team.

In the field of education, methods in the same direction have been studied as peer learning (the previous chapter, "[Seen from education](#ldd-education)"). Read in the words of this reader, both pair programming and peer learning are ways of working on the shape of the interactions between people before working on what gets built (programs or learning).

<details><summary>In more detail: the numbers on pair programming, and their limits</summary>

In the course experiment of Williams and colleagues, on the first assignment, the pairs spent on average 60% more person-hours than individuals. Once they were used to it, that difference fell to a minimum of 15%. The share passing the automated tests across the four assignments was 70–78% for individuals and 86–94% for pairs. The pairs said they worked "harder and smarter" because they did not want to let their partner down. Williams and colleagues write that many of the participants chose to pair themselves, and that what happens when it is forced on people who do not want it needs further study.

In the study by Plonka and colleagues, novices putting their own thinking into words, reflecting, and exploring were not seen in the data. The experienced side said that explaining in words adds an extra mental load, and the authors write that this may be one reason pair programming is more tiring than working alone.

Cockburn and Williams report from interviews that awkward teams, as they were encouraged to think out loud, began to enjoy and trust one another. They also write that they know of no study that has examined, with statistics, the effect on a team's communication. In Zuill's mob programming, the person holding the keyboard must listen to and trust the voices of the others, and they switch every 15 minutes by timer.

</details>

pjdhiro sees the field of computer science as something that could also be called an imitation of humans and organizations. In this chapter, taking easy-to-grasp examples such as AI as clues, we look from the viewpoint of computer science for shapes similar to this page's way of seeing.

🚧 Under construction.
